In particular, the following uniform bound holds
:

\displaystyle\inf_{0<\delta\lesssim 1}L_{\delta}(n)\gtrsim|n|

(2.8)

for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.

(iv)

Define
h(n,\delta)
by

\displaystyle L_{\delta}(n)=n^{2}-h(n,\delta)n^{2}.

(2.9)

Then, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}h^{2}(n,\delta)=\infty

(2.10)

for any
\delta>0
.

Proof.

From (
1.52
), we have
L_{\delta}(n)=\frac{3}{\delta}K_{\delta}(n)
.
Hence, from Lemma
2.1
, we have

L_{\delta}(n)>0
for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.
On the other hand,
from the Mittag-Leffler expansion
[
3
, (11) on p. 189]
, we have

\displaystyle\pi z\coth(\pi z)=\frac{\pi z}{i}\cot\Big(\frac{\pi z}{i}\Big)=1+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{2z^{2}}{k^{2}+z^{2}}

(2.11)

for
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus i\mathbb{Z}
.
Then, from (
1.52
) and (
2.11
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}L_{\delta}(n)&=6n^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k^{2}\pi^{2}+\delta^{2}n^{2}}\\
&=\frac{6n^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k^{2}}-6n^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\bigg(\frac{1}{k^{2}\pi^{2}}-\frac{1}{k^{2}\pi^{2}+\delta^{2}n^{2}}\bigg)\\
&=n^{2}-n^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{6\delta^{2}n^{2}}{k^{2}\pi^{2}(k^{2}\pi^{2}+\delta^{2}n^{2})}\end{split}

(2.12)

for any
\delta>0
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Hence, we conclude that
L_{\delta}(n)<n^{2}

for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.
This proves the claim (i).

From (
2.9
) and (
2.12
), we have

\displaystyle h(n,\delta)=6\delta^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{n^{2}}{k^{2}\pi^{2}(k^{2}\pi^{2}+\delta^{2}n^{2})},

(2.13)

which tends to
0
as
\delta\to 0
.
We also note that the expression after the first equality in (
2.12
)
shows that
L_{\delta}(n)
is monotonic in
\delta
.
This yields the claim (ii).
From (
2.13
),
we see that,
as
n\to\infty
,

h(n,\delta)\not\to 0
(for each fixed
\delta>0
),
which yields (
2.10
).
This proves the claim (iv).

Lastly, we prove (iii).
Suppose
\delta|n|\lesssim 1
.
Then, from (
2.12
), we have

\displaystyle L_{\delta}(n)

\displaystyle=6n^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k^{2}\pi^{2}+\delta^{2}n^{2}}\gtrsim n^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k^{2}+1}\gtrsim n^{2}.

(2.14)

Now, suppose

\delta|n|\gg 1
and
\delta\lesssim 1
.
Then, from (
2.12
) and a Riemann sum approximation, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \frac{L_{\delta}(n)}{|n|}&\gtrsim\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{|n|}{k^{2}\pi^{2}+\delta^{2}n^{2}}=\frac{1}{\delta}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\pi^{2}(\frac{k}{\delta|n|})^{2}+1}\frac{1}{\delta|n|}\\
&\gtrsim\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dx}{\pi^{2}x^{2}+1}\gtrsim 1.\end{aligned}

(2.15)

Note that the implicit constants in (
2.14
) and (
2.15
)
are independent of
\delta
.
This proves the claim (iii).
∎

2.2.
Tools from stochastic analysis

In the following, we review some basic facts on
the Hermite polynomials
and the Wiener chaos estimate.
See, for example,
[
52
,
63
]
.

We define the
k
th Hermite polynomials

H_{k}(x;\sigma)
with variance
\sigma
via the following generating function:

e^{tx-\frac{1}{2}\sigma t^{2}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{k}}{k!}H_{k}(x;\sigma)

(2.16)

for
t,x\in\mathbb{R}
and
\sigma>0
.
When
\sigma=1
, we set
H_{k}(x)=H_{k}(x;1)
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle H_{k}(x;\sigma)=\sigma^{\frac{k}{2}}H_{k}(\sigma^{-\frac{1}{2}}x).

(2.17)

It is well known that
\{H_{k}/\sqrt{k!}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}}

form an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}dx)
.
In the following, we list the first few Hermite polynomials
for readers’ convenience:

\displaystyle H_{0}(x;\sigma)=1,\qquad H_{1}(x;\sigma)=x,\qquad H_{2}(x;\sigma)=x^{2}-\sigma,

\displaystyle H_{3}(x;\sigma)=x^{3}-3\sigma x,\qquad H_{4}(x;\sigma)=x^{4}-6\sigma x^{2}+3\sigma^{2}.

From (
2.16
), we obtain
the following recursion relation:

\partial_{x}H_{k}(x;\sigma)=kH_{k-1}(x;\sigma)

for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and the following identity:

\displaystyle H_{k}(x+y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\begin{pmatrix}k\\
\ell\end{pmatrix}x^{k-\ell}H_{\ell}(y),

which, together with (
2.17
), yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}H_{k}(x+y;\sigma)&=\sigma^{\frac{k}{2}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\begin{pmatrix}k\\
\ell\end{pmatrix}\sigma^{-\frac{k-\ell}{2}}x^{k-\ell}H_{\ell}(\sigma^{-\frac{1}{2}}y)\\
&=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\begin{pmatrix}k\\
\ell\end{pmatrix}x^{k-\ell}H_{\ell}(y;\sigma).\end{split}

(2.18)

Let
\{g_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}

be an independent family of
standard complex-valued Gaussian random variables
conditioned that
g_{n}=\overline{g_{-n}}
.
We first recall the following bound:

\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle n\rangle^{-\varepsilon}|g_{n}|\leq C_{\varepsilon,\omega}<\infty

(2.19)

almost surely for some random constant
C_{\varepsilon,\omega}>0
;
see
Lemma 3.4 in
[
25
]
.
See also Appendix in
[
64
]
.

We define a real-valued, mean-zero Gaussian white noise
W
on
\mathbb{T}
by

\displaystyle W(x;\omega)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}g_{n}(\omega)e^{-in\cdot x}.

(2.20)

Next, we introduce the isonormal Gaussian process

\big\{W_{f}:f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T})\big\}

associated to the Gaussian white noise
W
.

Definition 2.4
.

The isonormal Gaussian process
\big\{W_{f}:f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T})\big\}

is a real-valued, mean-zero Gaussian process
indexed by the real separable Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})

such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[W_{f}W_{g}\big]=\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}_{x}}

for
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
.
Moreover, we can realize
W_{f}
as follows:

\displaystyle f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T})\longmapsto W_{f}=\langle f,W\rangle_{L^{2}_{x}}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{f}(n)g_{n}(\omega),

(2.21)

where
W
is as in (
2.20
).

Remark 2.5
.

The action (
2.21
) on
f
by the white noise
is referred to as the white noise functional in
[
74
,
69
]
.
Note that
W_{f}
is basically the ‘periodic’ Wiener integral on
\mathbb{T}
.

In the following, we denote
L^{2}(\Omega,\sigma\{W\},\mathbb{P})
the space of real-valued,
square-integrable random variables that are measurable with respect to
W
.
We present below a fundamental result in Gaussian analysis,
providing
us an
orthogonal decomposition of this
L^{2}
-probability space.
Let
\Gamma_{k}^{W}
be the
L^{2}(\Omega)
-completion
of the linear span of the set

\{H_{k}(W_{f}):f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T});\|f\|_{L^{2}}=1\}
.
We call
\Gamma_{k}^{W}
the
k
th Wiener chaos associated to
W
.
The following Wiener-Ito chaos decomposition holds:

\displaystyle L^{2}(\Omega,\sigma\{W\},\mathbb{P})=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}\Gamma_{k}^{W}.

(2.22)

The orthogonal decomposition (
2.22
) indicates that random variables belonging to
Wiener chaoses of different orders are uncorrelated (namely,
L^{2}(\Omega)
-orthogonal).
See also the following particular case that we will often use in our computations.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
Y_{1},Y_{2}
be two real-valued, mean-zero, and jointly Gaussian random variables with variances

\sigma_{1}=\mathbb{E}[Y_{1}^{2}]>0
and
\sigma_{2}=\mathbb{E}[Y_{2}^{2}]>0
.
Then, for
k,m\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[H_{k}(Y_{1};\sigma_{1})H_{m}(Y_{2};\sigma_{2})\big]=\mathbf{1}_{k=m}\cdot k!\big(\mathbb{E}[Y_{1}Y_{2}]\big)^{k}.

(2.23)

For example, with
f,h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
, the random variables

Y_{1}=W_{f}
and
Y_{2}=W_{h}
,
with
\sigma_{1}=\|f\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}
and
\sigma_{2}=\|h\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}

satisfy the identity (
2.23
).

Next, we state the Wiener chaos estimate,
which is a consequence of Nelson’s hypercontractivity
[
62
]
.
See, for example,
[
80
, Theorem I.22]
.
See also
[
83
, Proposition 2.4]
.

Lemma 2.7

(Wiener chaos estimate)
.

Let
\{g_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}

be an independent family of
standard complex-valued Gaussian random variables
conditioned that
g_{n}=\overline{g_{-n}}
.
Given
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let

\{Q_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of polynomials in

\mathbf{g}=\{g_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
of degrees at most
k

such that
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}Q_{j}(\mathbf{g})\in\mathbb{R}
,
almost surely.
Then, for any finite
p\geq 2
, we have

\bigg\|\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}Q_{j}(\mathbf{g})\bigg\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq(p-1)^{\frac{k}{2}}\bigg\|\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}Q_{j}(\mathbf{g})\bigg\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

Lastly,
we provide a brief discussion on
the Wick renormalization.

\bullet

Wick renormalization.

Let
\{\beta_{k},k\in\mathbb{N}\}
be independent real-valued standard Gaussian random variables,
which can be built from the Gaussian white noise
W
in (
2.20
).
Consider the polynomial
Q(x_{1},...,x_{n})

with
n
variables. We denote its degree by
{\rm deg}(Q)
.
Then, the random variable
Q(\beta_{1},...,\beta_{n})
belongs to
the sum of the first
{\rm deg}(Q)
Wiener chaoses, that is,

Q(\beta_{1},...,\beta_{n})\in\bigoplus_{k\leq{\rm deg}(Q)}\Gamma_{k}^{W}.

One can find a unique polynomial
P
with the same degree
and the same coefficient on the leading order term such that

P(\beta_{1},...,\beta_{n})\in\Gamma_{k}^{W}
,
that is,
P(\beta_{1},...,\beta_{n})
is the projection of
Q(\beta_{1},...,\beta_{n})

onto
\Gamma^{W}_{{\rm deg}(Q)}
.
We call such a polynomial
P
as the Wick-ordered version of
Q
,
and we write
P=\mathcal{W}(Q)
.

Example 2.8
.

(i)
Consider the polynomial
Q(x_{1},...,x_{n})=x_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{k_{n}}
.
Then, we have

\mathcal{W}(Q)(x_{1},...,x_{n})=\prod_{j=1}^{n}H_{k_{j}}(x_{j}),

where
H_{k}
is the
k
th Hermite polynomial with variance
\sigma=1
.

(ii)
Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
consider the following truncated random Fourier series
X_{\delta,N}
:

\displaystyle X_{\delta,N}(x,\omega)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ast}}\frac{g_{n}(\omega)}{|K_{\delta}(n)|^{\frac{1}{2}}}e_{n}.

(2.24)

Note that

X_{\delta,N}=\mathbf{P}_{N}X_{\delta}
, where
X_{\delta}
is as in (
1.41
).
For each
x\in\mathbb{T}
,

X_{\delta,N}(x)
is a real-valued, mean-zero Gaussian random variable
with variance
\sigma_{\delta,N}
in (
1.43
).
Then, the Wick-ordered version of
X_{\delta,N}^{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
is given by
\mathcal{W}(X_{\delta,N}^{k})=H_{k}(X_{\delta,N};\sigma_{\delta,N})
.
Compare this with (
1.44
).

2.3.
Various modes of convergence for probability measures
and random variables

We conclude this section by going over various modes of convergence
for probability measures
and random variables.
See, for example,
[
76
, Chapter 3]

and
[
85
, Chapter 2]

for further discussions.
See also
[
32
]
.

\bullet

Convergence in probability
and the Ky-Fan distance.

Let
X
and
Y
be two real-valued random variables
defined on a common probability space
\Omega
.
Then, the
Ky-Fan distance
between
X
and
Y

is defined by

\displaystyle d_{\rm KF}\big(X,Y\big)=\mathbb{E}\big[1\wedge|X-Y|\big],

where
a\wedge b:=\min(a,b)
.
It is known that the Ky-Fan distance
characterizes
convergence in probability.
Namely, a sequence
\{Z_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

of random variables converges in probability to some limit
Z

if and only if
d_{\rm KF}(Z_{n},Z)\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

The usual continuous mapping theorem
[
9
, Problem 5.17 on p. 83]

states that if a sequence
\{Z_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

converges to a limit
Z
in probability,
then, given a continuous function
\phi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\{\phi(Z_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

converges to
\phi(Z)
in probability.
For our purpose, we need to extend this continuous mapping
theorem
for

uniform
convergence in probability.

Lemma 2.9

(uniform continuous mapping theorem)
.

Let
\mathcal{J}\subset[0,\infty]
be an index set.
Suppose that
\{Z_{\delta,n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

converges in probability to a limit
Z_{\delta}

uniformly in
\delta\in\mathcal{J}
, as
n\to\infty
in the following sense
:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\delta\in\mathcal{J}}d_{\rm KF}(Z_{\delta,n},Z_{\delta})=0

(2.25)

or equivalently, for any
\eta>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\delta\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbb{P}\big(|Z_{\delta,n}-Z_{\delta}|>\eta\big)=0.

(2.26)

Suppose that the family of random variables
\{Z_{\delta}\}_{\delta\in\mathcal{J}}

is tight, meaning that for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a compact set
K_{\varepsilon}\subset\mathbb{R}

such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\delta\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbb{P}\big(Z_{\delta}\in K^{c}_{\varepsilon}\big)\leq\varepsilon.

(2.27)

Then,
given any continuous function
\phi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\delta\in\mathcal{J}}d_{\rm KF}\big(\phi(Z_{\delta,n}),\phi(Z_{\delta})\big)=0.

(2.28)

Note that the tightness assumption
on
\{Z_{\delta}\}_{\delta\in\mathcal{J}}
is crucial.

Proof.

Let us first show the equivalence of
(
2.25
) and
(
2.26
).
Let
0<\eta<1
.
Then,
by Markov’s inequality and (
2.25
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(|Z_{\delta,n}-Z_{\delta}|>\eta\big)\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbf{1}_{\{|Z_{\delta,n}-Z_{\delta}|>\eta\}}\tfrac{|Z_{\delta,n}-Z_{\delta}|\wedge\eta}{\eta}\Big]\leq\frac{1}{\eta}d_{\rm KF}(Z_{\delta,n},Z_{\delta})

and

\displaystyle d_{\rm KF}(Z_{\delta,n},Z_{\delta})=\mathbb{E}\big[|Z_{\delta,n}-Z_{\delta}|\wedge 1\big]\leq\eta+\mathbb{P}\big(|Z_{\delta,n}-Z_{\delta}|\geq\eta\big).

This proves the equivalence of (
2.25
) and
(
2.26
).

We now prove (
2.28
).
Fix
\beta>0
.
In view of (
2.27
), there exists
\eta=\eta(\beta)\geq 1
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\delta\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbb{P}\big(|Z_{\delta}|>\eta\big)\leq\beta.

(2.29)

Since
\phi
is continuous,
it is uniformly continuous
on
[-2\eta,2\eta]
.
In particular, there exists small

\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\phi,\beta)>0

such that

\displaystyle|\phi(x)-\phi(y)|\leq\beta,\quad\text{whenever $x,y\in[-2\eta,2\eta]$ with $|x-y|\leq\varepsilon$}.

(2.30)

Without loss of generality,
we assume that
\varepsilon\leq\eta
.
Note that these parameters
\varepsilon,\beta
, and
\eta
do not depend on
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

From (
2.26
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\delta\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(|\phi(Z_{\delta,n})-\phi(Z_{\delta})|\wedge 1\big)\mathbf{1}_{\{|Z_{\delta,n}-Z_{\delta}|>\varepsilon\}}\Big]\\
&\quad\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\delta\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbb{P}\big(|Z_{\delta,n}-Z_{\delta}|>\varepsilon\big)=0.\end{split}

(2.31)

On the other hand,
from (
2.30
) and (
2.29
),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}&\Big[\big(|\phi(Z_{\delta,n})-\phi(Z_{\delta})|\wedge 1\big)\mathbf{1}_{\{|Z_{\delta,n}-Z_{\delta}|\leq\varepsilon\}}\Big]\\
&\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(|\phi(Z_{\delta,n})-\phi(Z_{\delta})|\wedge 1\big)\mathbf{1}_{\{|Z_{\delta,n}-Z_{\delta}|\leq\varepsilon,|Z_{\delta}|\leq\eta\}}\Big]\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad+\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(|\phi(Z_{\delta,n})-\phi(Z_{\delta})|\wedge 1\big)\mathbf{1}_{\{|Z_{\delta}|>\eta\}}\Big]\leq 2\beta.\end{split}

(2.32)

Since
\beta>0
is arbitrary,
(
2.28
) follows from (
2.31
) and (
2.32
).
∎

\bullet

Convergence in total variation and the Hellinger distance.

Let
\mu
and
\nu
be two
probability measures on a measurable space
(E,\mathcal{E})
,
the
total variation distance

d_{\rm TV}
of

\mu
and
\nu
is given by

\displaystyle d_{\rm TV}(\mu,\nu):=\sup\big\{|\mu(A)-\nu(A)|:A\in\mathcal{E}\big\}.

(2.33)

This metric induces a much stronger topology than the one induced by the weak convergence.
15
15

15

For example, let
\mu_{N}
denote the
law of the random variable
\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}(Y_{1}+...+Y_{N})
,
where
Y_{i},i\in\mathbb{N}
, are i.i.d. random variables
with
\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}=1)=\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}=-1)=\frac{1}{2}
.
Then, the classical central limit theorem asserts that

\mu_{N}
converges weakly to the standard Gaussian measure on
\mathbb{R}
,
while due to the discrete nature of
\mu_{N}
, its total variation distance
from the standard Gaussian measure is always one.

Next, we recall the notion of
the Hellinger integral
[
26
,
29
]
.
Let
\mu
and
\nu
be two probability measures on
a measurable space
(E,\mathcal{E})
.
Note that both
\mu
and
\nu
are absolutely continuous with respect to the probability measure

\lambda=\frac{1}{2}(\mu+\nu)
.
Then,
the Hellinger integral of
\mu
and
\nu
is defined by

\displaystyle H(\mu,\nu)=\int_{E}\sqrt{\frac{d\mu}{d\lambda}\frac{d\nu}{d\lambda}}d\lambda.

(2.34)

In fact, the definition
(
2.34
) is independent of the choice of a probability measure

\lambda
such that
\mu,\nu\ll\lambda
.
When
\mu
and
\nu
are equivalent
(i.e. mutually absolutely continuous),
we can write
H(\mu,\nu)
as

\displaystyle H(\mu,\nu)=\int_{E}\sqrt{\frac{d\nu}{d\mu}}d\mu.

(2.35)

Note that
0\leq H(\mu,\nu)\leq 1
.
The Hellinger integral provides a
criterion for singularity (and equivalence) of two probability measures.
It is known

[
29
, Proposition 2.20]

that
H(\mu,\nu)=0
if and only if
\mu
and
\nu

are mutually singular.
Thus, for
\mu
and
\nu
to be equivalent,
we must have
H(\mu,\nu)>0
.
In fact, when
\mu
and
\nu
are product measures
on
(\mathbb{R}^{\infty},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{R}^{\infty}})
,
the condition
H(\mu,\nu)>0
is also sufficient
(Kakutani’s theorem). See Theorem 2.7 in
[
26
]
.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
\{\mu_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{\nu_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

be two sequences of probability measures on
(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{R}})

such that
\mu_{n}
and
\nu_{n}
are equivalent for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
\mu=\bigotimes_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mu_{n}

and
\nu=\bigotimes_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\nu_{n}
.
Then, we have
H(\mu,\nu)=\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}H(\mu_{n},\nu_{n})

and

•

H(\mu,\nu)>0
if and only if
\mu
and
\nu
are equivalent.
In this case,
we have

\displaystyle\frac{d\mu}{d\nu}=\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\nu_{n}}.

(2.36)

•

H(\mu,\nu)=0
if and only if
\mu
and
\nu
are mutually singular.

With the notations as above,
we introduce the
Hellinger distance

d_{\rm H}

of
\mu
and
\nu
by setting

\displaystyle\begin{split}d_{\rm H}(\mu,\nu)&=\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\int_{E}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{d\mu}{d\lambda}}-\sqrt{\frac{d\nu}{d\lambda}}\Big)^{2}d\lambda\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&=\big(1-H(\mu,\nu)\big)^{\frac{1}{2}},\end{split}

(2.37)

where
H(\mu,\nu)
is the Hellinger integral defined in (
2.35
).
It is clear that
0\leq d_{\rm H}(\mu,\nu)\leq 1
.
We state
Le Cam’s inequality, relating the total variation distance and Hellinger distance;
see
Lemma 2.3 in
[
85
]
.
16
16

16

Note a slightly difference multiplicative constant
in the definition of the Hellinger distance in
[
85
]
.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
d_{\rm TV}
and
d_{\rm H}
be as in (
2.33
) and (
2.37
), respectively.
Then, we have

\big(d_{\rm H}(\mu,\nu)\big)^{2}\leq d_{\rm TV}(\mu,\nu)\leq\sqrt{2}\cdot d_{\rm H}(\mu,\nu)

for any probability measures
\mu
and
\nu
on a measurable space
(E,\mathcal{E})
.
In particular, a sequence
\{\mu_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

of probability measures on
(E,\mathcal{E})
converges to some limit
\mu

in total variation if and only if it converges to the same limit in the Hellinger distance.

In the remaining part of the paper,
we do not make use of the Hellinger distance.
We, however, decided to introduce it here due to its connection
to the total variation distance and
also to the fact that Hellinger integral plays an important role in
the proof of Lemma
3.2
.
See also Remark
3.3
(iii).

\bullet

Kullback-Leibler divergence (= relative entropy).

We now define
the
Kullback-Leibler divergence

d_{\rm KL}(\mu,\nu)
between
\mu
and
\nu
by
setting

\displaystyle d_{\rm KL}(\mu,\nu)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\int_{E}\log\frac{d\mu}{d\nu}d\mu,&\text{if }\mu\ll\nu,\\
\infty,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(2.38)

which is nothing but the relative entropy of
\mu
with respect to
\nu
.
While the total variation distances and the Hellinger distance
are metrics, the Kullback-Leibler divergence is not a metric.
For example,
d_{\rm KL}(\cdot,\cdot)
is not symmetric, and moreover,
the symmetrized version
d_{\rm KL}(\mu,\nu)+d_{\rm KL}(\mu,\nu)
is not a metric,
either.
If
\mu
and
\nu
are product measures
of the form
\mu=\bigotimes_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mu_{n}

and
\nu=\bigotimes_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\nu_{n}
, then we have

\displaystyle d_{\rm KL}(\mu,\nu)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}d_{\rm KL}(\mu_{n},\nu_{n}).

(2.39)

The following lemma
shows that
convergence in the Kullback-Leibler divergence
(or in relative entropy)
implies convergence in total variation
and in the Hellinger distance.
See Lemmas 2.4 and 2.5 in
[
85
]

for the proof.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
d_{\rm TV},

d_{\rm H}
, and
d_{\rm KL}
be as in (
2.33
), (
2.37
), and (
2.38
), respectively.
Then, we have

\displaystyle d_{\rm H}(\mu,\nu)\leq\frac{\sqrt{d_{\rm KL}(\mu,\nu)}}{\sqrt{2}}

(2.40)

and

\displaystyle d_{\rm TV}(\mu,\nu)\leq\frac{\sqrt{d_{\rm KL}(\mu,\nu)}}{\sqrt{2}}.

(2.41)

The second inequality (
2.41
) is known as Pinsker’s inequality
and it is slightly stronger than

d_{\rm TV}(\mu,\nu)\leq\sqrt{d_{\rm KL}(\mu,\nu)}
, which follows from Lemma
2.11
and (
2.40
).

\bullet

Weak convergence the Lévy-Prokhorov metric.

Finally, let us introduce the Lévy-Prokhorov metric for
probability measures on a separable metric space
(\mathcal{M},d)
.
Given
\varepsilon>0
,
we define
an
\varepsilon
-neighborhood of
a measurable subset
A\subset\mathcal{M}
by

A^{\varepsilon}:=\big\{z\in\mathcal{M}:\,d(z,x)<\varepsilon\,\,\text{for some $x\in\mathcal{M}$}\big\}.

Given two probability measures
\mu
and
\nu
on
\mathcal{M}
,
their Lévy-Prokhorov distance
d_{\rm LP}(\mu,\nu)
is defined by

\displaystyle\begin{split}d_{\rm LP}(\mu,\nu):=\inf\big\{\varepsilon>0:\,&\mu(A)\leq\nu(A^{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon\ {\rm and}\ \nu(A)\leq\mu(A^{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon\\
&\text{for all measurable $A\subset\mathcal{M}$}\big\}.\end{split}

(2.42)

Note that the Lévy-Prokhorov metric is indeed a metric on
the space of probability measures on
\mathcal{M}
.
It is known that
the Lévy-Prokhorov metric
induces the same topology as the topology for weak convergence.
Together with this property,
we only need one additional property of the Lévy-Prokhorov metric in this paper,
that is, the triangle inequality; see (
5.33
) below.
See
[
10
,
32
]
and
[
6
, Section 30.3]

for a further discussion.

Lastly,
we recall the Prokhorov theorem
and the Skorokhod representation theorem.

Definition 2.13
.

Let
\mathcal{J}
be any nonempty index set.
A family

\{\rho_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{J}}
of probability measures
on a metric space
\mathcal{M}
is said to be tight
if, for every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a compact set
K_{\varepsilon}\subset\mathcal{M}

such that
\sup_{i\in\mathcal{J}}\rho_{i}(K_{\varepsilon}^{c})\leq\varepsilon
.
We say that
\{\rho_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{J}}
is relatively compact, if every sequence
in
\{\rho_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{J}}
contains a weakly convergent subsequence.

Note that the index set
\mathcal{J}
does not need to be countable.
We now
recall the following
Prokhorov theorem from
[
10
,
6
]
.

Lemma 2.14

(Prokhorov theorem)
.

If a sequence of probability measures
on a metric space
\mathcal{M}
is tight, then
it is relatively compact.
If in addition,
\mathcal{M}
is separable and complete, then relative compactness is
equivalent to tightness.

Lastly, we recall the following Skorokhod representation
theorem from
[
6
, Chapter 31]
.

Lemma 2.15

(Skorokhod representation theorem)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a complete
separable metric space
(
i.e. a Polish space
)
.
Suppose that
probability measures

\{\rho_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

on
\mathcal{M}
converges weakly to a probability measure
\rho

as
n\to\infty
.
Then, there exist a probability space
(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}})
,
and random variables
X_{n},X:\widetilde{\Omega}\to\mathcal{M}

such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(X_{n})=\rho_{n}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{L}(X)=\rho,

and
X_{n}
converges
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely to
X
as
n\to\infty
.

3.
Gibbs measures in the deep-water regime

In this section, we go over the construction of the Gibbs measures
for the gILW equation (
1.4
),
including the gBO case (
\delta=\infty
), and
prove convergence of the Gibbs measures in the deep-water limit
(as
\delta\to\infty
).
As mentioned in Section
1
,
we construct the Gibbs measure
as a weighted Gaussian measure,
where the base Gaussian measure is given by

\mu_{\delta}
in (
1.22
)
with the understanding that it is given by
\mu_{\infty}
in (
1.37
)
when
\delta=\infty
.
For
0<\delta<\infty
,
let
K_{\delta}(n)
be as in (
1.42
).
We extend the definition of
K_{\delta}(n)

to
\delta=\infty
by setting

\displaystyle K_{\infty}(n)=|n|,

(3.1)

which is consistent with Lemma
2.1
.
Then, a typical element
under the Gaussian measure
\mu_{\delta}
in (
1.22
)
(and
\mu_{\infty}
in (
1.37
))
is given by
X_{\delta}
in (
1.41
)
when
0<\delta<\infty

and
X_{\infty}:=X_{\text{\rm BO}}
in (
1.38
) when
\delta=\infty
.
It is easy to see that,
given
0<\delta\leq\infty
,
X_{\delta}\in H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})\setminus L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
for any
\varepsilon>0
, almost surely.
Indeed, from Lemma
2.1
,
we have

K_{\delta}(n)\sim_{\delta}|n|
.
Hence, with
X_{\delta,N}=\mathbf{P}_{N}X_{\delta}
in (
2.24
), it follows from Lemma
2.7
that
there exists
C_{\delta}>0
such that, for any finite
p\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\|X_{\delta,N}\|_{L^{p}_{\omega}H^{-\varepsilon}_{x}}\leq p^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\langle\nabla\rangle^{-\varepsilon}X_{\delta,N}(x)\|_{L^{2}_{x}L^{2}_{\omega}}\leq C_{\delta}\,p^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg(\sum_{0<|n|\leq N}\frac{1}{|n|^{1+2\varepsilon}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\sim C_{\delta}\,p^{\frac{1}{2}},

(3.2)

uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}
, provided that
\varepsilon>0
.
A similar computation together with the Borel-Cantelli lemma shows that
X_{\delta,N}

converges,
in
L^{p}(\Omega)
and almost surely, to the limit
X_{\delta}

in
H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})
for any
\varepsilon>0
.
The fact that

X_{\delta}\notin L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
almost surely
follows from
Lemma B.1 in
[
19
]
.

In Subsection
3.1
,
we first study various properties of the base Gaussian measures
\mu_{\delta}
.
See Proposition
3.1
.
By restricting our attention to the defocusing case (
k\in 2\mathbb{N}+1
),
we then go over the construction of the Gibbs measures in Subsection

3.2
.
In Subsection
3.3
,
we continue to study the defocusing case and establish
convergence in total variation
of the Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta}
to
\rho_{\text{\rm BO}}

in the deep-water limit (
\delta\to\infty
).
Finally, in Subsection
3.4
,
we present the proof of Theorem
1.3

when
k=2
.

3.1.
Equivalence of the base Gaussian measures

Proposition 3.1
.

(i)

Let
X_{\delta}
and
X_{\text{\rm BO}}
be as in (
1.41
) and (
1.38
), respectively.
Then, given any
\varepsilon>0

and finite
p\geq 1
,

X_{\delta}
converges to
X_{\text{\rm BO}}
in
L^{p}(\Omega;H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}))

and in
H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})
almost surely,
as
\delta\to\infty
.
In particular, the Gaussian measure
\mu_{\delta}
in (
1.22
)
converges weakly to the Gaussian measure
\mu_{\infty}
in (
1.37
), as
\delta\to\infty
.

(ii)

For any
0<\delta<\infty
,
the Gaussian measures
\mu_{\delta}
and
\mu_{\infty}
are equivalent.

(iii)

As
\delta\to\infty
,
the Gaussian measure
\mu_{\delta}
converges to
\mu_{\infty}

in the Kullback-Leibler divergence defined in (
2.38
).
In particular,
\mu_{\delta}
converges to
\mu_{\infty}
in total variation.

Part (iii) of Proposition
3.1

plays an essential role in establishing convergence in total variation
of the Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta}
to
\rho_{\text{\rm BO}}
in the deep-water limit (
\delta\to\infty
).

In proving Part (ii) of Proposition
3.1
, we resort to
the following Kakutani’s theorem
[
45
]
in the Gaussian
setting
(or the Feldman-Hájek theorem
[
33
,
42
]
;
see also
[
26
, Theorem 2.9]
).
See, for example,
[
20
,
73
,
75
,
39
]
,
where Kakutani’s theorem was used in the study of dispersive PDEs.
In particular, see also Proposition B.1 in
[
20
]
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\{A_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}
and
\{B_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}
be two sequences of independent,
real-valued, mean-zero Gaussian random variables with

\mathbb{E}[A_{n}^{2}]=a_{n}>0

and
\mathbb{E}[B_{n}^{2}]=b_{n}>0
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.
Then, the laws of the sequences
\{A_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}
and
\{B_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}
are equivalent
if and only if

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\Big(\frac{a_{n}}{b_{n}}-1\Big)^{2}<\infty.

(3.3)

If they are not equivalent, then they are singular.

We first present a short proof of Lemma
3.2
,
based on Lemma
2.10
.
See also the proof of Theorem 2.9 in
[
26
]
.

Proof of Lemma
3.2
.

Given
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
, let
\mu_{n}
and
\nu_{n}
denote the laws
of
A_{n}
and
B_{n}
, respectively,
and set

\mu=\bigotimes_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\mu_{n}
and
\nu=\bigotimes_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\nu_{n}
.
Namely,

\mu
are
\nu
are the laws of the sequences
\{A_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}
and
\{B_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}
,
respectively.
The Hellinger integral
H(\mu,\nu)
defined in (
2.35
) is given by an infinite product:

\displaystyle H(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle=\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}H(\mu_{n},\nu_{n})=\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sqrt{\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\nu_{n}}}d\nu_{n}

\displaystyle=\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}(a_{n}b_{n})^{\frac{1}{4}}}e^{-\frac{1}{4}(\frac{1}{a_{n}}+\frac{1}{b_{n}})x^{2}}dx

\displaystyle=\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{\sqrt{2}(a_{n}b_{n})^{\frac{1}{4}}}{\sqrt{a_{n}+b_{n}}}.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\big(H(\mu,\nu)\big)^{4}

\displaystyle=\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{4a_{n}b_{n}}{(a_{n}+b_{n})^{2}}=\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\bigg(1-\frac{(a_{n}-b_{n})^{2}}{(a_{n}+b_{n})^{2}}\bigg).

Hence,
H(\mu,\nu)>0
if and only if

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{(a_{n}-b_{n})^{2}}{(a_{n}+b_{n})^{2}}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\Big(\frac{a_{n}}{b_{n}}-1\Big)^{2}\bigg/\Big(\frac{a_{n}}{b_{n}}+1\Big)^{2}<\infty.

(3.4)

Note that the condition (
3.4
)
is equivalent to the
condition (
3.3
),
since if one of the sums in (
3.3
)
or (
3.4
) converges, then
\frac{a_{n}}{b_{n}}

must tend to
1
as
n\to\infty
,
which implies the other sum also converges.
Then, the desired conclusion follows from Lemma
2.10
.
∎

We now present the proof of Proposition
3.1
.

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

(i)
Let
\varepsilon>0
and fix finite
p\geq 1
.
Then, it follows from Lemma
2.7
, (
1.38
), (
1.41
),
and

\sqrt{a}-\sqrt{b}\leq\sqrt{a-b}
for any
a\geq b\geq 0

together with
(
2.1
) in Lemma
2.1
that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|X_{\delta}-X_{\text{\rm BO}}\|_{L^{p}_{\omega}H^{-\varepsilon}_{x}}&\lesssim_{p}\|\langle\nabla\rangle^{-\varepsilon}(X_{\delta}-X_{\text{\rm BO}})(x)\|_{L^{2}_{x}L^{2}_{\omega}}\\
&\sim\Bigg(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ast}}\frac{1}{\langle n\rangle^{2\varepsilon}}\bigg(\frac{1}{K_{\delta}^{\frac{1}{2}}(n)}-\frac{1}{|n|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\bigg)^{2}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\leq\bigg(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ast}}\frac{1}{\langle n\rangle^{2\varepsilon}}\frac{|n|-K_{\delta}(n)}{|n|K_{\delta}(n)}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\lesssim\bigg(\frac{1}{\delta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ast}}\frac{1}{\langle n\rangle^{2+2\varepsilon}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\delta^{\frac{1}{2}}}\longrightarrow 0,\end{split}

(3.5)

as
\delta\to\infty
.
See also (
2.3
)
for the penultimate step
in (
3.5
).

As for the almost sure convergence,
we repeat a computation analogous to (
3.5
)
but with (
2.19
) in place of
\mathbb{E}[|g_{n}|^{2}]\sim 1
.
Then, together with Lemma
2.1
(for
\delta\geq 2
),
we have

\displaystyle\big\|X_{\delta}(\omega)-X_{\text{\rm BO}}(\omega)\big\|_{H^{-\varepsilon}}^{2}\leq\frac{C_{\varepsilon_{0},\omega}}{\delta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ast}}\frac{\langle n\rangle^{2\varepsilon_{0}}}{\langle n\rangle^{1+2\varepsilon}(|n|-\frac{1}{2})}\longrightarrow 0,

(3.6)

as
\delta\to\infty
, provided that
0<\varepsilon_{0}<\varepsilon
.
Recalling that
\mu_{\delta}
and
\mu_{\infty}

are the laws of
X_{\delta}
and
X_{\text{\rm BO}}
,
we conclude weak convergence of
\mu_{\delta}
to
\mu_{\infty}
.
This proves (i).

(ii) Rewrite
X_{\delta}
in (
1.41
) (and in (
1.38
) when
\delta=\infty

with the understanding (
3.1
))
as

\displaystyle X_{\delta}(\omega)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\bigg(\frac{\Re g_{n}}{\pi K_{\delta}^{\frac{1}{2}}(n)}\cos(nx)-\frac{\Im g_{n}}{\pi K_{\delta}^{\frac{1}{2}}(n)}\sin(nx)\bigg).

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
, set

A_{n}=\frac{\Re g_{n}}{\pi K_{\delta}^{\frac{1}{2}}(n)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad A_{-n}=-\frac{\Im g_{n}}{\pi K_{\delta}^{\frac{1}{2}}(n)},

and

B_{n}=\frac{\Re g_{n}}{\pi|n|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad B_{-n}=-\frac{\Im g_{n}}{\pi|n|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\qquad\text{for $\delta=\infty$}

with
a_{\pm n}=\mathbb{E}[A_{\pm n}^{2}]=\frac{1}{\pi K_{\delta}(n)}
and
b_{\pm n}=\mathbb{E}[B_{\pm n}^{2}]=\frac{1}{\pi|n|}
.
Then, from
Lemma
2.1
,
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\Big(\frac{a_{n}}{b_{n}}-1\Big)^{2}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ast}}\frac{(|n|-K_{\delta}(n))^{2}}{K^{2}_{\delta}(n)}\lesssim C_{\delta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ast}}\frac{1}{n^{2}}<\infty.

Therefore, the claimed equivalence of
\mu_{\delta}
and
\mu_{\infty}

follows from
Kakutani’s theorem (Lemma
3.2
).

(iii) In this part, we prove that
\mu_{\delta}
converges to

\mu_{\infty}
in the Kullback-Leibler divergence defined in (
2.38
).
Once this is achieved,
convergence in total variation follows from
Pinsker’s inequality ((
2.41
) in Lemma
2.12
).

Let us first write
\mu_{\delta}
,
0<\delta\leq\infty
, as the product
of Gaussian measures on
\mathbb{R}
(see also (
1.31
)):

\displaystyle d\mu_{\delta}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\bigotimes_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{K_{\delta}^{\frac{1}{2}}(n)}{\sqrt{2}\pi}e^{-\frac{1}{2\pi}K_{\delta}(n)(\Re\widehat{u}(n))^{2}}d\Re\widehat{u}(n)\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg(\bigotimes_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{K_{\delta}^{\frac{1}{2}}(n)}{\sqrt{2}\pi}e^{-\frac{1}{2\pi}K_{\delta}(n)(\Im\widehat{u}(n))^{2}}d\Im\widehat{u}(n)\bigg)

with the identification (
3.1
) when
\delta=\infty
.
With
x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we then have

\displaystyle d\mu_{\delta}

\displaystyle=\bigotimes_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{K_{\delta}(n)}{2\pi^{2}}e^{-\frac{1}{2\pi}K_{\delta}(n)|x|^{2}}dx=:\bigotimes_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{K_{\delta}(n)}{2\pi^{2}}d\mu_{\delta}^{n}.

(3.7)

Then,
the Radon-Nikodym derivative
\frac{d\mu_{\delta}^{n}}{d\mu_{\infty}^{n}}

is given by

\displaystyle\frac{d\mu_{\delta}^{n}}{d\mu_{\infty}^{n}}=\frac{K_{\delta}(n)}{n}e^{\frac{1}{2\pi}(n-K_{\delta}(n))|x|^{2}}.

(3.8)

See (
2.36
).
Then, from
Part (ii),
(
2.38
), and (
2.39
) with (
3.7
) and (
3.8
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}d_{\rm KL}(\mu_{\delta},\mu_{\infty})&=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}d_{\rm KL}(\mu_{\delta}^{n},\mu_{\infty}^{n})\\
&=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\bigg(\log\frac{K_{\delta}(n)}{n}+\frac{1}{2\pi}(n-K_{\delta}(n))|x|^{2}\bigg)\frac{K_{\delta}(n)}{2\pi^{2}}e^{-\frac{1}{2\pi}K_{\delta}(n)|x|^{2}}dx\\
&=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\bigg(\log\frac{K_{\delta}(n)}{n}+\frac{1}{2\pi}(n-K_{\delta}(n))\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{K_{\delta}(n)}{2\pi^{2}}|x|^{2}e^{-\frac{1}{2\pi}K_{\delta}(n)|x|^{2}}dx\bigg)\\
&=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\phi\bigg(\frac{n}{K_{\delta}(n)}\bigg),\end{split}

(3.9)

where
\phi(t):=t-1-\log t
.
Note that
\phi(1)=0
and
\phi^{\prime}(t)>0
for
t>1
.
Then, it follows from Lemma
2.1

that for each fixed
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
we have

\displaystyle\phi\bigg(\frac{n}{K_{\delta}(n)}\bigg)\text{ decreases to }\phi(1)=0,

(3.10)

as
\delta\to\infty
,
since
\frac{n}{K_{\delta}(n)}
decreases to 1 as
\delta\to\infty
.
Hence, if the right-hand side of (
3.9
) is finite
for some
\delta\gg 1
,
then the observation (
3.10
) allows us to apply
the dominated convergence theorem
and conclude

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to\infty}d_{\rm KL}(\mu_{\delta},\mu_{\infty})=\lim_{\delta\to\infty}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\phi\bigg(\frac{n}{K_{\delta}(n)}\bigg)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\lim_{\delta\to\infty}\phi\bigg(\frac{n}{K_{\delta}(n)}\bigg)=0,

yielding the desired convergence in the Kullback-Leibler divergence.

It remains to check that the right-hand side of (
3.9
) is
finite for some
\delta\gg 1
.
In fact, we show that the right-hand side of (
3.9
) is
finite for any
\delta>0
.
By a direct computation, we have
\phi(t)\leq(t-1)^{2}
for
t\geq 1
.
Then, from Lemma
2.1
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\phi\bigg(\frac{n}{K_{\delta}(n)}\bigg)\leq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{(n-K_{\delta}(n))^{2}}{K_{\delta}^{2}(n)}\leq C_{\delta}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{n^{2}}<\infty

for any
\delta>0
.
This concludes the proof of Proposition
3.1
.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

(i)
By using the Wiener chaos estimate (Lemma
2.7
),
Chebyshev’s inequality, and the Borel-Cantelli lemma,
one can easily upgrade the convergence of

X_{\delta}
to
X_{\text{\rm BO}}
to that in
L^{2}(\Omega;W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))

and in
W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}(\mathbb{T})
almost surely,

(ii)
From (
3.5
),
we see that
the difference
X_{\delta}-X_{\text{\rm BO}}

lives in
H^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})
,
17
17

17

In fact, in
W^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon,\infty}(\mathbb{T})

if we use the Wiener chaos estimate (Lemma
2.7
),
although
neither

X_{\delta}
nor
X_{\text{\rm BO}}
belongs to
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
.

(iii)
In order to prove convergence of
\mu_{\delta}
to
\mu_{\infty}

in total variation,
it is indeed possible to
directly show that

\mu_{\delta}
converges to
\mu_{\infty}
in
the Hellinger distance
d_{\rm H}
defined in (
2.37
)
and invoke Lemma
2.11
.

3.2.
Construction of the Gibbs measure for the defocusing gILW equation

In this subsection, we present the construction of the Gibbs measure
for the gILW equation (
1.4
),
0<\delta\leq\infty

with the understanding that the
\delta=\infty
case corresponds
to the gBO (
1.12
),
in the defocusing case:
k\in 2\mathbb{N}+1
.
We treat the
k=2
case, corresponding to the ILW equation (
1.1
),
in Subsection
3.4
.
Our basic strategy is to follow the argument
presented in
[
74
]
on the construction of the complex
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-measures,
by utilizing the Wiener chaos estimate (Lemma
2.7
)
and Nelson’s estimate.
In order to establish convergence of the Gibbs measures
in the deep-water limit (
\delta\to\infty
),
however, we need to establish an
L^{p}(\Omega)
-integrability
of the (truncated) densities,

uniformly in both the frequency-truncation parameter
N\in\mathbb{N}

and the depth parameter
\delta\gg 1
.
See Proposition
3.6
.
This uniform bound also plays a crucial role
in the dynamical part presented in Section
5
.

Fix the depth parameter
0<\delta\leq\infty
.
Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
X_{\delta,N}=\mathbf{P}_{N}X_{\delta}
,
where
X_{\delta}
is defined in (
1.41
):

\displaystyle X_{\delta,N}(\omega):=\mathbf{P}_{N}X_{\delta}(\omega)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{0<|n|\leq N}\frac{g_{n}(\omega)}{K_{\delta}^{\frac{1}{2}}(n)}e_{n}

with the identification (
3.1
) when
\delta=\infty
.
When
\delta=\infty
,
we also set

X_{\text{\rm BO},N}:=X_{\infty,N}=\mathbf{P}_{N}X_{\infty}=\mathbf{P}_{N}X_{\text{\rm BO}},

where
X_{\text{\rm BO}}
is as in (
1.38
).
Given
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
\mathcal{W}(X_{\delta,N}^{k})=H_{k}(X_{\delta,N};\sigma_{\delta,N})

denotes the Wick power defined in
(
1.44
), where
\sigma_{\delta,N}
is as in (
1.43
).
Then, the truncated Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta,N}
in (
1.45
)
can be written as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\rho_{\delta,N}(A)&=Z_{\delta,N}^{-1}\int_{H^{-\varepsilon}}\mathbf{1}_{\{u\in A\}}e^{-\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(u_{N}^{k+1})dx}d\mu_{\delta}(u)\\
&=Z_{\delta,N}^{-1}\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{\delta}(\omega)\in A\}}e^{-\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(X_{\delta,N}^{k+1}(\omega))dx}d\mathbb{P}(\omega)\end{split}

(3.11)

for any measurable set
A\subset H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})
with some small
\varepsilon>0
.
where
u_{N}=\mathbf{P}_{N}u
.
In the following, we freely interchange
the representations in terms of
X_{\delta}

and in terms of
u
distributed by
\mu_{\delta}
,
when there is no confusion.

Let us first
construct the limiting Wick power
\mathcal{W}(X^{k}_{\delta})

and the related stochastic objects.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
0<\delta\leq\infty
.
Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let

\mathcal{W}(X_{\delta,N}^{k})
be as in (
1.44
).
Then, given any finite
p\geq 1
,
the sequence
\{\mathcal{W}(X_{\delta,N}^{k})\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

is Cauchy in
L^{p}(\Omega;W^{s,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))
,
s<0
,
thus converging to a limit, denoted by

\mathcal{W}(X_{\delta}^{k})
.
This convergence of
\mathcal{W}(X_{\delta,N}^{k})

to
\mathcal{W}(X_{\delta}^{k})
also holds almost surely in
W^{s,\infty}(\mathbb{T})
.
Furthermore, given any finite
p\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\big\|\|\mathcal{W}(X_{\delta,N}^{k})\|_{W^{s,\infty}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}<\infty

(3.12)

and

\displaystyle\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\big\|\|\mathcal{W}(X_{\delta,M}^{k})-\mathcal{W}(X_{\delta,N}^{k})\|_{W^{s,\infty}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\longrightarrow 0

(3.13)

for any
M\geq N
, tending to
\infty
.
In particular, the rate of convergence is uniform in
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.

As a corollary, the following two statements hold.

(i)

Let

0<\delta\leq\infty
.
Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
R_{\delta,N}(u)=R_{\delta,N}(u;k+1)
denotes the truncated potential energy defined by

\displaystyle R_{\delta,N}(u):=\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}((\mathbf{P}_{N}u)^{k+1})dx=\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}H_{k+1}\big(\mathbf{P}_{N}u;\sigma_{\delta,N}\big)dx,

(3.14)

where
\sigma_{\delta,N}
is as in (
1.43
)
with the identification (
3.1
) when
\delta=\infty
;
see
\sigma_{\infty,N}
in (
3.51
).
Then, given any finite
p\geq 1
,
the sequence

\{R_{\delta,N}(u)\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

converges to the limit
:

\displaystyle R_{\delta}(u)=\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(u^{k+1})dx=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}((\mathbf{P}_{N}u)^{k+1})dx

(3.15)

in
L^{p}(d\mu_{\delta})
, as
N\to\infty
.
Furthermore,
there exists
\theta>0
such that given any finite
p\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}}\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\|R_{\delta,N}(u)\|_{L^{p}(d\mu_{\delta})}<\infty,

(3.16)

with
R_{\delta,\infty}(u)=R_{\delta}(u)
,
and

\displaystyle\|R_{\delta,M}(u)-R_{\delta,N}(u)\|_{L^{p}(d\mu_{\delta})}\leq\frac{C_{k,\delta}\,p^{\frac{k+1}{2}}}{N^{\theta}}

(3.17)

for
any
M\geq N\geq 1
.
For
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
,
we can choose the constant
C_{k,\delta}
in (
3.17
) to be independent of
\delta

and hence the rate of convergence
of
R_{\delta,N}(u)
to the limit
R_{\delta}(u)
is uniform in
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.

(ii)

Let

0<\delta\leq\infty
.
Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
F_{N}(u)=F_{N}(u;k)
be the truncated renormalized nonlinearity
in (
1.70
) given
by

\displaystyle F_{N}(u):=\partial_{x}\mathbf{P}_{N}\mathcal{W}((\mathbf{P}_{N}u)^{k})=\partial_{x}\mathbf{P}_{N}H_{k}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u;\sigma_{\delta,N}),

(3.18)

where
\sigma_{\delta,N}
is as in (
1.43
)
with the identification (
3.1
) when
\delta=\infty
;
see
\sigma_{\infty,N}
in (
3.51
).
Then, given any finite
p\geq 1
,
the sequence
\{F_{N}(u)\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

is Cauchy in
L^{p}(d\mu_{\delta};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
,
s<-1
,
thus converging to a limit denoted by

F(u)=\partial_{x}\mathcal{W}(u^{k})
.
Furthermore, given any finite
p\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}}\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\big\|\|F_{N}(u)\|_{H^{s}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}(d\mu_{\delta})}<\infty,

(3.19)

with
F_{\infty}(u)=F(u)
,
and

\displaystyle\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\big\|\|F_{M}(u)-F_{N}(u)\|_{H^{s}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}(d\mu_{\delta})}\longrightarrow 0

(3.20)

for any
M\geq N
, tending to
\infty
.
In particular, the rate of convergence
of
F_{N}(u)
to the limit
F(u)

is uniform in
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.

Remark 3.5
.

In the proof of Proposition
3.4
,
we use (
2.3
) to obtain a lower bound
on
K_{\delta}(n)
, uniformly in
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
, for any fixed
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.
The lower bound
\delta=2
is by no means sharp.
For example, in view of
the strict monotonicity of
K_{\delta}(n)
in
\delta\geq 1
(for fixed
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
)
and the fact that
K_{\delta}(n)\neq 0
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}

as stated in Lemma
2.1
,
a slight modification of the proof of Proposition
3.4
yields
the uniform (in
\delta
) bounds
for
1\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
Since our main interest is to take the limit
\delta\to\infty
,
we do not attempt to optimize a lower bound for
\delta
.
The same comment applies to
the subsequent results presented in this section
and hence to Theorem
1.3
.

Proof of Proposition
3.4
.

Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
and

x,y\in\mathbb{T}
,
we define
\gamma_{N}=\gamma_{N}(\delta)
by setting

\displaystyle\gamma_{N}(x-y):=\mathbb{E}[X_{\delta,N}(x)X_{\delta,N}(y)]=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{0<|n|\leq N}\frac{e_{n}(x-y)}{K_{\delta}(n)}.

(3.21)

Note that we have

\displaystyle\gamma_{N}(x-y)=\mathbb{E}[X_{\delta,N}(x)X_{\delta,M}(y)]

for any
M\geq N\geq 1
.
In the following,
for simplicity of notation,
we set
u_{N}=\mathbf{P}_{N}u

and suppress the
\delta
-dependence
in
\gamma_{N}=\gamma_{N}(\delta)
.

Let us first make a preliminary computation.
Given
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathcal{F}(H_{k}(X_{\delta,N};\sigma_{\delta,N}))(n)\,\overline{\mathcal{F}(H_{k}(X_{\delta,N};\sigma_{\delta,N}))(m)}\Big]\\
&=\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\big[H_{k}(X_{\delta,N}(x);\sigma_{\delta,N})H_{k}(X_{\delta,N}(y);\sigma_{\delta,N})\big]e_{-n+m}(x)e_{-m}(x-y)dydx.\end{split}

(3.22)

From Lemma
2.6
with (
3.21
)
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[H_{k}(X_{\delta,N}(x);\sigma_{\delta,N})H_{k}(X_{\delta,N}(y);\sigma_{\delta,N})\big]=k!\,\gamma_{N}^{k}(y-x).

(3.23)

Then,
from (
3.22
), (
3.23
), a change of variables
z=y-x
,
and integrating in
x
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}&\Big[\mathcal{F}(H_{k}(X_{\delta,N};\sigma_{\delta,N}))(n)\,\overline{\mathcal{F}(H_{k}(X_{\delta,N};\sigma_{\delta,N}))(m)}\Big]\\
&=k!\int_{\mathbb{T}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{T}}e_{-n+m}(x)dx\bigg)\gamma_{N}^{k}(z)e_{m}(z)dz\\
&=2\pi k!\mathbf{1}_{n=m}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{T}}\gamma_{N}^{k}(z)e_{n}(z)dz.\end{split}

(3.24)

Fix small
\varepsilon>0
.
Then, by Sobolev’s inequality
with finite
r\gg 1
such that
r\varepsilon>1
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{W}(u_{N}^{k})\|_{W^{s,\infty}}\lesssim\|\mathcal{W}(u_{N}^{k})\|_{W^{s+\varepsilon,r}}.

(3.25)

Let
p\geq r
.
Then, by (
3.25
), Minkowski’s inequality
(with
p\geq r\gg 1
),
the Wiener chaos estimate (Lemma
2.7
),
(
3.24
), and
the boundedness of the torus
\mathbb{T}
,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big\|&\|\mathcal{W}(u_{N}^{k})\|_{W^{s,\infty}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}(d\mu_{\delta})}\lesssim p^{\frac{k}{2}}\big\|\|\langle\nabla\rangle^{s+\varepsilon}\mathcal{W}(u_{N}^{k})\|_{L^{2}(d\mu_{\delta})}\big\|_{L^{r}_{x}}\\
&=\frac{p^{\frac{k}{2}}}{2\pi}\bigg\|\Big\|\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle n\rangle^{s+\varepsilon}\mathcal{F}(H_{k}(X_{\delta,N};\sigma_{\delta,N}))(n)e_{n}(x)\Big\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\bigg\|_{L^{r}_{x}}\\
&=C_{k}\,p^{\frac{k}{2}}\bigg(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle n\rangle^{2(s+\varepsilon)}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\gamma_{N}^{k}(z)e_{n}(z)dz\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.\end{split}

(3.26)

From (
3.21
) with
\gamma_{N}^{k}(z)=\gamma_{N}^{k}(-z)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}\gamma_{N}^{k}(z)e_{n}(z)dz=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{k-1}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<|n_{j}|\leq N\\
j=1,\dots,k\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbf{1}_{n=n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}}}{\prod_{j=1}^{k}K_{\delta}(n_{j})}.

(3.27)

Hence, from (
3.26
) and (
3.27
),
and Lemma
2.1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big\|&\|\mathcal{W}(u_{N}^{k})\|_{W^{s,\infty}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}(d\mu_{\delta})}\\
&\leq C_{k,\delta}\,p^{\frac{k}{2}}\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<|n_{j}|\leq N\\
j=1,\dots,k\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\prod_{j=1}^{k}\langle n_{j}\rangle}\langle n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}\rangle^{2(s+\varepsilon)}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\leq C_{k,\delta}\,p^{\frac{k}{2}}\bigg(\sum_{n_{1},...,n_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{1}{\prod_{j=1}^{k}\langle n_{j}\rangle}\langle n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}\rangle^{2(s+\varepsilon)}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}<\infty,\end{split}

(3.28)

uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
provided that
s+\varepsilon<0
.
This last condition can be guaranteed for
s<0

by taking
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small.
In view of (
2.3
),
the bound (
3.28
) holds uniformly in
2\leq\delta\leq\infty

(namely, the constant
C_{k,\delta}
can be chosen to be independent
of
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
).
This proves (
3.12
).

Let
M\geq N\geq 1
and
p\geq 2
.
Proceeding as above, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big\|&\|\mathcal{W}(u_{M}^{k})-\mathcal{W}(u_{N}^{k})\|_{W^{s,\infty}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}(d\mu_{\delta})}\\
&\leq C_{k}\,p^{\frac{k}{2}}\bigg(\sum_{n}\langle n\rangle^{2(s+\varepsilon)}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\big(\gamma_{M}^{k}(z)-\gamma_{N}^{k}(z)\big)e_{n}(z)dz\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\leq C_{k,\delta}\,p^{\frac{k}{2}}\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<|n_{j}|\leq M\\
j=1,\dots,k\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\prod_{j=1}^{k}\langle n_{j}\rangle}\langle n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}\rangle^{2(s+\varepsilon)}\\
&\hphantom{XXXXXXX}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<|n_{j}|\leq N\\
j=1,\dots,k\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\prod_{j=1}^{k}\langle n_{j}\rangle}\langle n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}\rangle^{2(s+\varepsilon)}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\leq C_{k,\delta}\,p^{\frac{k}{2}}\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<|n_{j}|\leq M\\
j=1,\dots,k\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbf{1}_{\max_{j=1,\dots,k}|n_{j}|>N}}{\prod_{j=1}^{k}\langle n_{j}\rangle}\langle n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}\rangle^{2(s+\varepsilon)}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\leq C_{k,\delta}\,p^{\frac{k}{2}}N^{\max(s,-\frac{1}{2})+2\varepsilon}\end{split}

(3.29)

for any
\varepsilon>0
,
provided that
s<0
.
By choosing
0<2\varepsilon<\min\big(-s,\frac{1}{2}\big)
,
we then obtain

\displaystyle\big\|\|\mathcal{W}(u_{M}^{k})-\mathcal{W}(u_{N}^{k})\|_{W^{s,\infty}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}(d\mu_{\delta})}\longrightarrow 0,

(3.30)

as
N\to\infty
.
In view of (
2.3
),
the bound (
3.29
) holds uniformly in
2\leq\delta\leq\infty

and thus the convergence in (
3.30
)
holds uniformly in
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
,
yielding (
3.13
).

By applying Chebyshev’s inequality
(see also Lemma 4.5 in
[
87
]
),
to (
3.29
) (with
M=\infty
)
and summing over in
N\in\mathbb{N}

we have

\displaystyle\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\bigg(\|\mathcal{W}(u^{k})-\mathcal{W}(u_{N}^{k})\|_{W^{s,\infty}}>\frac{1}{j}\bigg)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}e^{-cN^{-\frac{2}{k}(\max(s,-\frac{1}{2})+2\varepsilon)}j^{-\frac{2}{k}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{-c^{\prime}j^{-\frac{2}{k}}}<\infty.

Therefore, we conclude from the Borel-Cantelli lemma that
there exists
\Omega_{j}
with
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{j})=1

such that
for each
\omega\in\Omega_{j}
,
there exists
N_{j}=N_{j}(\omega)\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\|\mathcal{W}(u^{k})(\omega)-\mathcal{W}(u_{N}^{k}))(\omega)\|_{W^{s,\infty}}<\frac{1}{j}

for any
N\geq N_{j}
.
By setting
\Sigma=\bigcap_{j=1}^{\infty}\Omega_{j}
,
we have
\mathbb{P}(\Sigma)=1
.
Hence, we conclude that
\mathcal{W}(u_{N}^{k})
converges almost surely to
\mathcal{W}(u^{k})
in
W^{s,\infty}(\mathbb{T})
.

Let us briefly discuss how to obtain the corollaries (i) and (ii).
We only discuss the difference estimates (
3.18
) and (
3.20
).
The first corollary on
R_{\delta,N}(u)
(Part (i))
easily follows from the discussion above (in particular (
3.29
) with
k
replaced by
k+1
) by noting that

|R_{\delta,M}(u)-R_{\delta,N}(u)|\leq C_{k}\|\mathcal{W}(u_{M}^{k+1})-\mathcal{W}(u_{N}^{k+1})\|_{H^{s}}

for any
s<0
. We can take
s=-\frac{1}{2}
for example.

As for the second corollary on
F_{N}(u)
,
we just need to note that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big\|&\|F_{M}(u)-F_{N}(u)\|_{H^{s}}\big\|_{L^{p}(d\mu_{\delta})}\\
&\leq\big\|\|(\mathbf{P}_{M}-\mathbf{P}_{N})\mathcal{W}(u_{M}^{k})\|_{H^{s+1}}\big\|_{L^{p}(d\mu_{\delta})}\\
&\quad+\big\|\|\mathcal{W}(u_{M}^{k})-\mathcal{W}(u_{N}^{k})\|_{H^{s+1}}\big\|_{L^{p}(d\mu_{\delta})}\\
&=:\hskip 1.42262pt\text{I}\hskip 1.42262pt+\text{I \hskip-7.96677pt I}.\end{split}

(3.31)

For
s<-1
,
we can estimate
I I
in (
3.31
) just as in (
3.29
).
As for the first term
I
in (
3.31
), we note that
due to the projection
\mathbf{P}_{M}-\mathbf{P}_{N}
, we have

|n|=|n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}|>N
in
a computation analogous to (
3.28
),
which in particular implies

\max_{j=1,\dots,k}|n_{j}|\gtrsim_{k}N
.
Hence, a slight modification of (
3.30
) yields the desired bound
(
3.20
).
∎

Next, we study the densities
for the truncated Gibbs measures
\rho_{\delta,N}
in (
3.11
).
As mentioned above, we restrict our attention to the defocusing case
in this subsection.
Namely, we fix
k\in 2\mathbb{N}+1
.
See Subsection
3.4

for the
k=2
case.
Given
0<\delta\leq\infty

and
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
G_{\delta,N}(u)
be the truncated density
defined in (
1.46
).
Our main goal is to establish an
L^{p}
-integrability of
the truncated density

G_{\delta,N}(u)

for the following two purposes:

•

In order to construct the limiting Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta}

for each fixed
0<\delta\leq\infty

(Theorem
1.3
(i)),
we establish such an

L^{p}
-integrability of
the truncated density,
uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}
but for each fixed
0<\delta\leq\infty
.

•

In order to prove convergence of the Gibbs measures
in the deep-water limit (Theorem
1.3
(ii)),
we establish
an

L^{p}
-integrability of
the truncated density,
uniformly in
both
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\delta\gg 1
.

Here, we need to study the
L^{p}
-integrability of
G_{\delta,N}(u)

with respect to the Gaussian measure
\mu_{\delta}
in (
1.22
),
which is different for different values of
\delta
.
In order to establish a uniform (in
\delta
) bound,
it is therefore more convenient to work with the Gaussian process
X_{\delta}

and the underlying probability measure
\mathbb{P}
on
\Omega
.

Given
0<\delta\leq\infty

and
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
we define
G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})=G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta};k+1)
by

\displaystyle G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})=e^{-R_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})}=e^{-\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(X_{\delta,N}^{k+1})dx},

where
R_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})=R_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta};k+1)

is the truncated potential energy defined
in (
3.14
).

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
k\in 2\mathbb{N}+1

and fix finite
p\geq 1
.
Given any
0<\delta\leq\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\|G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\|G_{\delta,N}(u)\|_{L^{p}(d\mu_{\delta})}\leq C_{p,k,\delta}<\infty.

(3.32)

In addition, the following uniform bound holds for
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\|G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}&=\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\|G_{\delta,N}(u)\|_{L^{p}(d\mu_{\delta})}\\
&\leq C_{p,k}<\infty.\end{split}

(3.33)

Define
G_{\delta}(X_{\delta})=G_{\delta,\infty}(X_{\delta})
by

G_{\delta}(X_{\delta})=e^{-R_{\delta}(X_{\delta})}

with
R_{\delta}(X_{\delta})
as in (
3.15
).
Then,

G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})
converges to
G_{\delta}(X_{\delta})

in
L^{p}(\Omega)
.
Namely,
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\|G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})-G_{\delta}(X_{\delta})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=0.

(3.34)

Furthermore,
the convergence is uniform in
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
:

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\|G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})-G_{\delta}(X_{\delta})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=0.

(3.35)

As a consequence, the uniform bounds (
3.32
)
and (
3.33
) hold even if we replace the supremum in
N\in\mathbb{N}

by the supremum in
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
.

Theorem
1.3
(i)
follows as a directly corollary to
Proposition
3.6
,
allowing us to define the limiting
Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta}
in (
1.48
).
Fix
0<\delta\leq\infty
.
Then, (
3.34
) with
p=1

implies that the partition function

Z_{\delta,N}=\|G_{\delta,N}(u)\|_{L^{1}(d\mu_{\delta})}

of the truncated Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta,N}
in (
1.45
)
converges to
the partition function

Z_{\delta}=\|G_{\delta}(u)\|_{L^{1}(d\mu_{\delta})}

of the Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta}
in (
1.48
).
Let
\mathcal{B}_{H^{-\varepsilon}}
denote
the collection of Borel sets in
H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})
.
Then, once again from (
3.34
),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{A\in\mathcal{B}_{H^{-\varepsilon}}}|\rho_{\delta,N}(A)-\rho_{\delta}(A)|\\
&\quad=\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{A\in\mathcal{B}_{H^{-\varepsilon}}}\bigg|\frac{Z_{\delta,N}}{Z_{\delta}}\rho_{\delta,N}(A)-\rho_{\delta}(A)\bigg|\\
&\quad\leq Z_{\delta}^{-1}\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{A\in\mathcal{B}_{H^{-\varepsilon}}}\int_{H^{-\varepsilon}}\mathbf{1}_{A}(u)|G_{\delta,N}(u)-G_{\delta}(u)|d\mu_{\delta}(u)\\
&\quad\leq Z_{\delta}^{-1}\lim_{N\to\infty}\|G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})-G_{\delta}(X_{\delta})\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\\
&\quad=0.\end{split}

(3.36)

This proves convergence in total variation of
\rho_{\delta,N}

to
\rho_{\delta}
.
By using (
3.35
) in place of (
3.34
),
a slight modification of the argument above yields uniform convergence
in total variation
of
\rho_{\delta,N}
to
\rho_{\delta}
for
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
See (
3.55
) below.
We omit details.

We now present the proof of
Proposition
3.6
.

Proof of Proposition
3.6
.

We break the proof into two steps.

\bullet

Step 1:
We first prove the uniform
L^{p}
-bounds
(
3.32
) and (
3.33
).
Given
k\in 2\mathbb{N}+1
,
the Hermite polynomial
H_{k+1}
has a global minimum;
there exists finite
a_{k+1}>0
such that

H_{k+1}(x)\geq-a_{k+1}
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
. It follows from (
2.17
)
that

\displaystyle H_{k+1}(x;\sigma)\geq-\sigma^{\frac{k+1}{2}}a_{k+1}

(3.37)

for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
\sigma>0
.
Hence, from (
3.14
) and (
3.37
)
with (
1.43
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} -R_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})&=-\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}H_{k+1}(X_{\delta,N};\sigma_{\delta,N})dx\\
&\leq\frac{2\pi}{k+1}\sigma_{\delta,N}^{\frac{k+1}{2}}a_{k+1}\leq A_{k,\delta}(\log(N+1))^{\frac{k+1}{2}}\end{aligned}

(3.38)

for some
A_{k,\delta}>0
,
uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
The bound (
3.38
) is exactly
where the defocusing nature of the equation plays a crucial role.

Remark 3.7
.

Recall the uniform lower bound
(
2.3
) for
2\leq\delta\leq\infty

(with the identification (
3.1
) when
\delta=\infty
).
In view of (
1.43
),
we can then choose
A_{k,\delta}
to be independent of

2\leq\delta\leq\infty

(and
N\in\mathbb{N}
)
as in the proof of Proposition
3.4
.
Similarly,
by restricting our attention to
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
,
we can choose the constant

c_{k,\delta}
in (
3.39
) below to be
independent of
2\leq\delta\leq\infty

since the constant
C_{k,\delta}
in (
3.17
) is independent of

2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
As a result, the constants
in

B_{k,\delta,p}

and
C_{2}(k,\delta,p)

in (
3.43
) below
can be chosen to be
independent of
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.

By applying
Proposition
3.4
(i)
and Chebyshev’s inequality
(see also Lemma 4.5 in
[
87
]
),
we have, for some
C_{1}>0
and
c_{k,\delta}>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(p|R_{\delta,M}(X_{\delta})-R_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})|>\lambda\Big)\leq C_{1}e^{-c_{k,\delta}\,p^{-\frac{2}{k+1}}N^{\frac{2\theta}{k+1}}\lambda^{\frac{2}{k+1}}}

(3.39)

for any
M\geq N\geq 1

and any
p,\lambda>0
.

By writing

\displaystyle\|G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\Big(e^{-pR_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})}>\alpha\Big)d\alpha

\displaystyle\leq 1+\int_{1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\Big(-pR_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})>\log\alpha\Big)d\alpha,

we see that the desired bound (
3.32
) follows once we show
that there exist
C_{2}=C_{2}(k,\delta,p)>0
and
\beta>0

such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(-pR_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})>\log\alpha\Big)\leq C_{2}\alpha^{-(1+\beta)}

(3.40)

for any
\alpha>1
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
We prove (
3.40
) via a standard application of
the so-called Nelson’s estimate.
Namely, given
\alpha>1
,
we choose
N_{0}=N_{0}(\alpha)>0

and establish (
3.40
)
for
N\geq N_{0}
and
N<N_{0}

in two different ways.

Given
\lambda:=\log\alpha>0
,
we choose
N_{0}>0

by setting

\displaystyle\lambda=2pA_{k,\delta}(\log(N_{0}+1))^{\frac{k+1}{2}}.

(3.41)

Then,
from (
3.38
) and (
3.41
), we have

\displaystyle-pR_{\delta,N_{0}}(X_{\delta})\leq pA_{k,\delta}(\log(N_{0}+1))^{\frac{k+1}{2}}=\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda.

(3.42)

Hence, from (
3.42
) and (
3.39
),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbb{P}\Big(&-pR_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})>\lambda\Big)\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(-p\big(R_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})-R_{\delta,N_{0}}(X_{\delta})\big)>\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda\Big)\\
&\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(p\big|R_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})-R_{\delta,N_{0}}(X_{\delta})\big|>\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda\Big)\\
&\leq C_{1}e^{-c^{\prime}_{k,\delta}\,p^{-\frac{2}{k+1}}N_{0}^{\frac{2\theta}{k+1}}\lambda^{\frac{2}{k+1}}}\\
&\leq C_{1}e^{-c^{\prime}_{k,\delta}\,p^{-\frac{2}{k+1}}\lambda^{\frac{2}{k+1}}(e^{B_{k,\delta,p}\lambda^{\frac{2}{k+1}}}-1)}\\
&\leq C_{2}(k,\delta,p)e^{-(1+\beta)\lambda}\end{aligned}

(3.43)

for any
N\geq N_{0}
.
On the other hand, for
N<N_{0}
, it follows from (
3.38
)
and (
3.41
) that

\displaystyle-pR_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})\leq pA_{k,\delta}(\log(N+1))^{\frac{k+1}{2}}<\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda

and thus we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(

\displaystyle-pR_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})>\lambda\Big)=0.

(3.44)

Putting (
3.43
) and (
3.44
) together, we conclude that
(
3.40
) holds for any
\alpha>1
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Therefore, we obtain

\displaystyle\|G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C_{3}(k,\delta,p)<\infty

(3.45)

for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

For
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
,
it follows from Remark
3.7

that the constant

C_{3}(k,\delta,p)
in (
3.45
)
can be chosen to be independent of
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
,
thus yielding (
3.33
).

\bullet

Step 2:
Next, we show the (uniform)
L^{p}
-convergence
of the truncated densities.

Fix
0<\delta\leq\infty
.
The
L^{p}
-convergence (
3.34
) of the truncated density
G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})

follows from the uniform bound (
3.32
) and a standard argument (see
[
86
, Remark 3.8]
). More precisely, as a consequence of Proposition
3.4
(i)
and the continuous mapping theorem,
we see that
G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})=e^{-R_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})}
converges in probability
to the limit
G_{\delta}(X_{\delta})=e^{-R_{\delta}(X_{\delta})}
.
Then, the
L^{p}
-convergence (
3.34
)
follows from the uniform bound (
3.32
) and this softer convergence in probability.
While we omit details of the argument in this case,
we present details of an analogous argument in
establishing the uniform
L^{p}
-convergence (
3.35
) in the following.

In the following, we present the proof of (
3.35
)
and thus restrict our attention to
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
Proposition
3.4
(i),
the continuity of the exponential function,
and the uniform continuous mapping theorem (Lemma
2.9
),
18
18

18

Here, we use the tightness of
\{R_{\delta}(X_{\delta})\}_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}
,
coming from (
3.16
),
to verify the hypothesis (
2.27
)
in Lemma
2.9
.

we see that

G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})

converges in probability to

G_{\delta}(X_{\delta})
as
N\to\infty
, uniformly in
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
Then, by setting

A_{\delta,N,\varepsilon}=\big\{|G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})-G_{\delta}(X_{\delta})|\leq\varepsilon\big\},

(3.46)

we have

\displaystyle\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\mathbb{P}({A^{c}_{\delta,N,\varepsilon}})\longrightarrow 0,

(3.47)

as
N\to\infty
.
Then, from (
3.46
),
Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequality,
the uniform (in
\delta
and
N
, including
N=\infty
) bound (
3.33
), and (
3.47
),
we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}

\displaystyle\|G_{\delta}(X_{\delta})-G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\|(G_{\delta}(X_{\delta})-G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta}))\cdot\mathbf{1}_{A_{\delta,N,\varepsilon}}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\quad+\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\|(G_{\delta}(X_{\delta})-G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta}))\cdot\mathbf{1}_{{A^{c}_{\delta,N,\varepsilon}}}\,\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon+\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\|G_{\delta}(X_{\delta})-G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})\|_{L^{2p}(\Omega)}\cdot\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\mathbb{P}\big(A^{c}_{\delta,N,\varepsilon}\big)^{\frac{1}{2p}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\varepsilon

for sufficiently large
N\gg 1
.
This proves
the uniform (in
\delta
)
L^{p}
-convergence (
3.35
).
This concludes the proof of Proposition
3.6
.
∎

3.3.
Convergence of the Gibbs measures in the deep-water limit

In this subsection, we present the proof of Theorem
1.3
(ii).
Once again, we restrict our attention to the defocusing case:
k\in 2\mathbb{N}+1
.
The construction of the Gibbs measures in the previous subsection
shows that, for each

0<\delta\leq\infty
, the Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta}
and the base
Gaussian measure
\mu_{\delta}
are equivalent.
On the other hand,
from Proposition
3.1
,
we know that the Gaussian measures
\mu_{\delta}

are all equivalent for
0<\delta\leq\infty
.
Therefore,
we conclude that the Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta}
,
0<\delta<\infty
,
for the defocusing gILW equation (
1.4
)
and the Gibbs measure
\rho_{\text{\rm BO}}=\rho_{\infty}

for the defocusing gBO equation (
1.12
) are equivalent.
This proves the first claim in Theorem
1.3
(ii).
Hence, it remains to show that
the Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta}
converges
to
\rho_{\text{\rm BO}}
in total variation, as
\delta\to\infty
.

Before proceeding to the proof of convergence in total variation
of
\rho_{\delta}
to
\rho_{\text{\rm BO}}
,
let us first present the following

L^{p}
-convergence of the (truncated) densities.
For
0<\delta\leq\infty
,
let
X_{\delta}
and
X_{\text{\rm BO}}=X_{\infty}

be as in (
1.41
) and (
1.38
), respectively,
and
let
R_{\delta}(X_{\delta})

(and
G_{\delta}(X_{\delta})
, respectively)
be the limit of

R_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})

constructed in Proposition
3.4

(and of
G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})

constructed in Proposition
3.6
, respectively).

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
k\in 2\mathbb{N}+1
and
1\leq p<\infty
. Then, given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to\infty}\|G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})-G_{\infty,N}(X_{\text{\rm BO}})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=0.

(3.48)

As a corollary, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to\infty}\|G_{\delta}(X_{\delta})-G_{\infty}(X_{\text{\rm BO}})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=0.

(3.49)

In particular, the partition function

Z_{\delta}
of the Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta}
in (
1.48
)
converges to the partition function
Z_{\text{\rm BO}}=Z_{\infty}

of the Gibbs measure
\rho_{\text{\rm BO}}=\rho_{\infty}
, as
\delta\to\infty
.

Remark 3.9
.

In view of the argument presented in
(
3.36
), one may be tempted to
conclude
directly from (
3.49
) in
Lemma
3.8

that
\rho_{\delta}
converges
to
\rho_{\text{\rm BO}}=\rho_{\infty}
in total variation
as
\delta\to\infty
.
However, this is not possible.
This is due to the fact that the base Gaussian measures

\mu_{\delta}
and
\mu_{\infty}
are different.
If we were to mimic the argument in (
3.36
),
the integral in the third step of (
3.36
)
would be replaced by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\Omega}&\Big(\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{\delta}\in A\}}G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})-\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{\text{\rm BO}}\in A\}}G_{\infty}(X_{\text{\rm BO}})\Big)d\mathbb{P}\\
&=\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{\delta}\in A\}}\Big(G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})-G_{\infty}(X_{\text{\rm BO}})\Big)d\mathbb{P}\\
&\quad+\int_{\Omega}\Big(\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{\delta}\in A\}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{\text{\rm BO}}\in A\}}\Big)G_{\infty}(X_{\text{\rm BO}})d\mathbb{P}.\end{split}

(3.50)

While we can apply (
3.49
) in Lemma
3.8

to control the first term on the right-hand side of (
3.50
),
we can not handle the second term as it is.
Note that the difference

\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{\delta}\in A\}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{\text{\rm BO}}\in A\}}

with respect to the
\mathbb{P}
-integration
(and taking the supremum in
A\in\mathcal{B}_{H^{-\varepsilon}}
)
is closely related to
the convergence in total variation of
\mu_{\delta}
to
\mu_{\infty}

proven in Proposition
3.1
(iii),
which plays a crucial role in the proof of
convergence in total variation of
\rho_{\delta}
to
\rho_{\text{\rm BO}}

presented below.

Proof of Lemma
3.8
.

Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
From (
1.43
)
and
Lemma
2.1
, we have

\displaystyle\sigma_{\delta,N}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{0<|n|\leq N}\frac{1}{K_{\delta}(n)}\longrightarrow\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{|n|\leq N}\frac{1}{|n|}=:\sigma_{\infty,N},

(3.51)

as
\delta\to\infty
.
It also follows from the definitions (
1.38
), (
1.41
), and Lemma
2.1

that, for any
x\in\mathbb{T}
and
\omega\in\Omega
,
19
19

19

Here,
we use the convention that
g_{n}(\omega)\in\mathbb{C}
for every
\omega\in\Omega
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.

X_{\delta,N}(x)
converges to
X_{\text{\rm BO},N}(x)
as
\delta\to\infty
.
Moreover,
from (
1.41
) and Lemma
2.1
,
we have

\displaystyle|X_{\delta,N}(x;\omega)|\lesssim\sum_{0<|n|\leq N}\frac{|g_{n}(\omega)|}{K_{\delta}^{\frac{1}{2}}(n)}\leq C_{N,\omega}<\infty

for any
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
,
x\in\mathbb{T}
, and
\omega\in\Omega
.
Then, by the
dominated convergence theorem applied to the integration in
x\in\mathbb{T}
,
we have

\displaystyle R_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta}(\omega))=\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}H_{k}(X_{\delta,N}(x;\omega);\sigma_{\delta,N})dx\longrightarrow R_{\infty,N}(X_{\rm BO}(\omega))

(3.52)

as
\delta\to\infty
, for any
\omega\in\Omega
.
As a consequence, we see that

G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta}(\omega))
converges to

G_{\infty,N}(X_{\rm BO}(\omega))

as
\delta\to\infty
,
for any
\omega\in\Omega
.
Moreover, from the uniform (in
\omega
) bound (
3.38
),
we conclude that

G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})
converges to
G_{\infty,N}(X_{\text{\rm BO}})
in
L^{p}(\Omega)
,
as
\delta\to\infty
.
This proves (
3.48
).

By the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|&G_{\delta}(X_{\delta})-G_{\infty}(X_{\rm BO})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\\
&\leq\|G_{\delta}(X_{\delta})-G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+\|G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})-G_{\infty,N}(X_{\text{\rm BO}})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\\
&\quad+\|G_{\infty,N}(X_{\text{\rm BO}})-G_{\infty}(X_{\rm BO})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}.\end{aligned}

(3.53)

Then, by first applying (
3.48
) above
and then (
3.35
) in Proposition
3.6

to (
3.53
)
(namely, we first take

\delta\to\infty

and then
N\to\infty
), we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to\infty}\|G_{\delta}(X_{\delta})-G_{\infty}(X_{\rm BO})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq 2\lim_{N\to\infty}\Big(\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\|G_{\delta}(X_{\delta})-G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\hphantom{XXXXXXX}+\lim_{\delta\to\infty}\|G_{\delta,N}(X_{\delta})-G_{\infty,N}(X_{\text{\rm BO}})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad=0.

This proves (
3.49
).
∎

We are now ready to show that the Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta}
in (
1.48
)
converges to
\rho_{\text{\rm BO}}=\rho_{\infty}
in total variation as
\delta\to\infty
.
By the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle d_{\rm TV}(\rho_{\delta},\rho_{\text{\rm BO}})\leq d_{\rm TV}(\rho_{\delta},\rho_{\delta,N})+d_{\rm TV}(\rho_{\delta,N},\rho_{\infty,N})+d_{\rm TV}(\rho_{\infty,N},\rho_{\text{\rm BO}})

(3.54)

for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
From Theorem
1.3
(i)
(see also Proposition
3.6
),
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}d_{\rm TV}(\rho_{\delta,N},\rho_{\delta})=0.

(3.55)

Hence, it suffices to prove

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to\infty}d_{\rm TV}(\rho_{\delta,N},\rho_{\infty,N})=0

(3.56)

for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Indeed, by applying (
3.55
) and (
3.56
) to (
3.54
)
(namely, by first taking
\delta\to\infty
and then
N\to\infty
), we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to\infty}d_{\rm TV}(\rho_{\delta},\rho_{\text{\rm BO}})

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{N\to\infty}\Big(\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}d_{\rm TV}(\rho_{\delta,N},\rho_{\delta})+\lim_{\delta\to\infty}d_{\rm TV}(\rho_{\delta,N},\rho_{\infty,N})\Big)

\displaystyle=0.

In the following, we prove (
3.56
) for any fixed
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then, Lemma
3.8
with
p=1

implies that the partition function

Z_{\delta,N}=\|G_{\delta,N}(u)\|_{L^{1}(d\mu_{\delta})}

of the truncated Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta,N}
in (
1.45
)
converges to
the partition function

Z_{\infty,N}=\|G_{\infty,N}(u)\|_{L^{1}(d\mu_{\infty})}

of the truncated Gibbs measure
\rho_{\infty,N}

for the gBO equation as
\delta\to\infty
.
Then, from Proposition
3.1
(ii),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lim_{\delta\to\infty}\sup_{A\in\mathcal{B}_{H^{-\varepsilon}}}|\rho_{\delta,N}(A)-\rho_{\infty,N}(A)|\\
&=\lim_{\delta\to\infty}\sup_{A\in\mathcal{B}_{H^{-\varepsilon}}}\bigg|\frac{Z_{\delta,N}}{Z_{\infty,N}}\rho_{\delta,N}(A)-\rho_{\infty,N}(A)\bigg|\\
&\leq Z_{\infty,N}^{-1}\lim_{\delta\to\infty}\sup_{A\in\mathcal{B}_{H^{-\varepsilon}}}\bigg|\int_{H^{-\varepsilon}}\mathbf{1}_{A}(u)\Big(G_{\delta,N}(u)\frac{d\mu_{\delta}}{d\mu_{\infty}}(u)-G_{\infty,N}(u)\Big)d\mu_{\infty}(u)\bigg|\\
&\leq Z_{\infty,N}^{-1}\lim_{\delta\to\infty}\int_{H^{-\varepsilon}}|G_{\delta,N}(u)-G_{\infty,N}(u)|\,d\mu_{\infty}(u)\\
&\quad+Z_{\infty,N}^{-1}\lim_{\delta\to\infty}\int_{H^{-\varepsilon}}G_{\delta,N}(u)\Big|\frac{d\mu_{\delta}}{d\mu_{\infty}}(u)-1\Big|\,d\mu_{\infty}(u).\end{split}

(3.57)

From (
1.46
) and
(
3.51
), we see that
G_{\delta,N}(u)

converges to
G_{\infty,N}(u)

\mu_{\infty}
-almost surely, as
\delta\to\infty
.
Moreover, it follows from (
3.38
)
and Remark
3.7

that

\displaystyle|G_{\delta,N}(u)-G_{\infty,N}(u)|\leq C_{N}<\infty

(3.58)

for any
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
Hence, by the dominated convergence theorem, we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to\infty}\int_{H^{-\varepsilon}}|G_{\delta,N}(u)-G_{\infty,N}(u)|\,d\mu_{\infty}(u)=0.

(3.59)

By Scheffé’s theorem (Lemma 2.1 in
[
85
]
;
see also Proposition 1.2.7 in
[
51
]
), we have

\displaystyle d_{\rm TV}(\mu_{\delta},\mu_{\infty})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{H^{-\varepsilon}}\Big|\frac{d\mu_{\delta}}{d\mu_{\infty}}(u)-1\Big|\,d\mu_{\infty}(u).

(3.60)

Then, it follows from
the convergence in total variation of
\mu_{\delta}
to
\mu_{\infty}

as
\delta\to\infty
(Proposition
3.1
(iii)), (
3.60
),
and the uniform (in
\delta
) bound (
3.38
) (and Remark
3.7
) for
2\leq\delta\leq\infty

that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lim_{\delta\to\infty}\int_{H^{-\varepsilon}}G_{\delta,N}(u)\Big|\frac{d\mu_{\delta}}{d\mu_{\infty}}(u)-1\Big|\,d\mu_{\infty}(u)\\
&\quad\leq C_{N}\lim_{\delta\to\infty}\int_{H^{-\varepsilon}}\Big|\frac{d\mu_{\delta}}{d\mu_{\infty}}(u)-1\Big|\,d\mu_{\infty}(u)\\
&\quad=2C_{N}\lim_{\delta\to\infty}d_{\rm TV}(\mu_{\delta},\mu_{\infty})\\
&\quad=0.\end{split}

(3.61)

Therefore, from (
3.57
), (
3.59
), and (
3.61
),
we conclude (
3.56
) and hence
convergence in total variation of
\rho_{\delta}
to
\rho_{\text{\rm BO}}
as
\delta\to\infty
.
This concludes the proof of Theorem
1.3

when
k\in 2\mathbb{N}+1
.

3.4.
Gibbs measures for the ILW equation: variational approach

We conclude this section
by presenting the proof of Theorem
1.3

for the
k=2
case, corresponding to the
ILW equation (
1.1
).
In this case, the problem is no longer defocusing
and thus
we need to consider the (truncated) Gibbs measures
with a Wick-ordered
L^{2}
-cutoff
of the form (
1.49
)
and (
1.50
).
As pointed out in Remark
1.4
,
there is no need for a renormalization
on the potential energy under the current (spatial) mean-zero condition.

Fix
K>0
in the remaining part of this section.
Given
0<\delta\leq\infty
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
define
the truncated density
G_{\delta,N}^{K}(u)
by

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{\delta,N}^{K}(u)&=\chi_{K}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(u_{N}^{2})dx\bigg)e^{-\frac{1}{3}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{N}^{3}dx}\\
&=\chi_{K}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{T}}H_{2}(u_{N};\sigma_{\delta,N})dx\bigg)e^{-\frac{1}{3}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{N}^{3}dx},\end{split}

(3.62)

where
u_{N}=\mathbf{P}_{N}u

and

\chi_{K}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]

is a continuous function such that
\chi_{K}(x)=1
for
|x|\leq K

and
\chi_{K}(x)=0
for
|x|\geq 2K
.

In view of the discussion
in Subsections
3.2

and
3.3
,
Theorem
1.3
for
k=2
follows
once we prove the following uniform bounds.

Proposition 3.10
.

Fix finite
p\geq 1
and
K>0
.
Then, given any
0<\delta\leq\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\|G_{\delta,N}^{K}(X_{\delta})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\|G_{\delta,N}^{K}(u)\|_{L^{p}(d\mu_{\delta})}\leq C_{p,\delta,K}<\infty.

In addition, the following uniform bound holds for
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
:

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\|G_{\delta,N}^{K}(X_{\delta})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\|G_{\delta,N}^{K}(u)\|_{L^{p}(d\mu_{\delta})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,K}<\infty.

Let us first discuss how to conclude Theorem
1.3

when
k=2
, by assuming
Proposition
3.10
.
Define the limiting density
G^{K}_{\delta}(u)
by

\displaystyle G_{\delta}^{K}(u)=\chi_{K}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(u^{2})dx\bigg)e^{-\frac{1}{3}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u^{3}dx}.

(3.63)

Note that Proposition
3.4
(i)
guarantees that

\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(u^{2})dx

and
\int_{\mathbb{T}}u^{3}dx=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(u^{3})dx
in (
3.63
)
exist as the limits in
L^{p}(\mu_{\delta})
of the truncated versions.
Hence, the truncated density

G_{\delta,N}^{K}(u)
converges in measure to
the limiting density
G^{K}_{\delta}(u)
in (
3.63
).
Hence, once we prove
Proposition
3.10
,
we can repeat the argument in Step 2 in the proof of
Proposition
3.6

to show the following convergence results.

Corollary 3.11
.

Let
0<\delta\leq\infty
and
1\leq p<\infty
.
Then,

G_{\delta,N}^{K}(X_{\delta})
converges to
G_{\delta}^{K}(X_{\delta})

in
L^{p}(\Omega)
as
N\to\infty
.
Namely,
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\|G_{\delta,N}^{K}(X_{\delta})-G_{\delta}^{K}(X_{\delta})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=0.

Furthermore,
the convergence is uniform in
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
:

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\|G_{\delta,N}^{K}(X_{\delta})-G_{\delta}^{K}(X_{\delta})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=0.

This proves an analogue of Theorem
1.3
(i) when
k=2
.
The equivalence
of the Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta}
in (
1.50
),
0<\delta<\infty
,
and
\rho_{\text{\rm BO}}
in (
1.39
)
follows from (i) the equivalence
of the Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta}

and the Gaussian measure with the Wick-ordered
L^{2}
-cutoff:

\displaystyle\chi_{K}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(u^{2})dx\bigg)d\mu_{\delta}(u)

(including
\delta=\infty
with the understanding that
\rho_{\infty}=\rho_{\text{\rm BO}}
),
and (ii) the equivalence of the base Gaussian measure

\mu_{\delta}
,
0<\delta\leq\infty
(Proposition
3.1
(ii)).

Finally, we discuss convergence of the Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta}

to
\rho_{\text{\rm BO}}

in the deep-water limit (
\delta\to\infty
).
In the defocusing case discussed in the previous subsection,
the bound (
3.38
) provided
the uniform (in
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
and
\omega\in\Omega)

bound on the truncated density
G_{\delta,N}(u)
;
see the discussion
after (
3.52
).
See also
(
3.58
) and
(
3.61
).
In the current non-defocusing case, however,
the bound (
3.38
) is not available to us. Nonetheless,
in view of (
1.44
)
and (
1.43
) with Lemma
2.1
,
the Wick-ordered
L^{2}
-cutoff in (
3.62
) with (
1.44
)
implies

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{N}^{2}dx\bigg|\leq\sigma_{\delta,N}+2K\leq C_{N,K}<\infty,

(3.64)

for any
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
where
C_{N,K}
is independent of
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
Then, by Sobolev’s inequality with (
3.64
), we have

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{N}^{3}dx\bigg|\lesssim\|u_{N}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{6}}}^{3}\leq N^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{N,K}^{\frac{3}{2}},

(3.65)

which provides a bound on the truncated density

G_{\delta,N}^{K}(u)
in (
3.62
), uniformly in
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
With this bound on

G_{\delta,N}^{K}(u)
,
we can repeat the argument presented in Subsection
3.3

to conclude the desired convergence in total variation of
\rho_{\delta}
to
\rho_{\text{\rm BO}}
as
\delta\to\infty
.

In the remaining part of this section, we present the proof of
Proposition
3.10
.
Given
0<\delta\leq\infty
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
, set

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{R}_{\delta,N}(u)&=\frac{1}{3}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{N}^{3}dx+A\,\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(u_{N}^{2})dx\bigg|^{2},\end{split}

(3.66)

where

\mathcal{W}(u_{N}^{2})=\mathcal{W}_{\delta,N}(u_{N}^{2})=H_{2}(u_{N};\sigma_{\delta,N})
.
Then,
as in
[
71
]
,
we consider the following truncated density:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{G}_{\delta,N}^{K}(u)&=e^{-\mathcal{R}_{\delta,N}(u)}=e^{-\frac{1}{3}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{N}^{3}dx-A|\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(u_{N}^{2})dx|^{2}}\end{split}

(3.67)

for some suitable
A>0
.
Noting that

\displaystyle\chi_{K}(x)\leq\exp\big(-A|x|^{\gamma}\big)\exp(A2^{\gamma}K^{\gamma})

(3.68)

for any
K,A,\gamma>0
,
we have

\displaystyle G_{\delta,N}^{K}(u)\leq C_{A,K}\cdot\mathcal{G}_{\delta,N}^{K}(u).

Hence,
Proposition
3.10
follows once we prove the following
uniform bounds
on
\mathcal{G}_{\delta,N}^{K}(u)
.

Proposition 3.12
.

Fix finite
p\geq 1
.
Then, there exists
