A_{0}=A_{0}(p)>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta,N}^{K}(X_{\delta})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta,N}^{K}(u)\|_{L^{p}(d\mu_{\delta})}\leq C_{p,\delta,K,A}<\infty

(3.69)

for any
0<\delta\leq\infty
,

K>0
,
and
A\geq A_{0}
.
In addition, the following uniform bound holds for
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta,N}^{K}(X_{\delta})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}&=\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta,N}^{K}(u)\|_{L^{p}(d\mu_{\delta})}\\
&\leq C_{p,K,A}<\infty\end{split}

(3.70)

for any
K>0
and
A\geq A_{0}
.

As mentioned in the introduction,
we employ the variational approach, introduced by Barashkov and Gubinelli
[
5
]
,
to prove Proposition
3.12
.
In particular,
we follow closely the argument in
[
71
]
,
where the
\delta=\infty
case was treated via the variational approach.
See also

[
39
,
70
,
66
,
16
,
67
]

for recent works on dispersive PDEs, where the variational approach played a crucial role.

Let us first introduce some notations.
Let
W(t)
be a cylindrical Brownian motion in

\displaystyle L^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{T})=\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}L^{2}(\mathbb{T})

of mean-zero functions on
\mathbb{T}
,
where
\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}
denotes the projection onto the non-zero frequencies.
Namely, we have

\displaystyle W(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}B_{n}(t)e_{n},

(3.71)

where

\{B_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}
is a sequence of mutually independent complex-valued
20
20

20

By convention, we normalize
B_{n}
such that
\text{Var}(B_{n}(t))=2\pi t
.

Brownian motions such that

\overline{B_{n}}=B_{-n}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.
Then, we define a centered Gaussian process
Y_{\delta}(t)

by

\displaystyle Y_{\delta}(t)=(\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x})^{-\frac{1}{2}}W(t),

(3.72)

where
({\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
is the Fourier multiplier operator
with the multiplier
(K_{\delta}(n))^{-\frac{1}{2}}

with
K_{\delta}(n)
as in (
1.42
).
In view of (
1.41
), we have

\mathcal{L}(Y_{\delta}(1))=\mu_{\delta}
.
Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
we set
Y_{\delta,N}=\mathbf{P}_{N}Y_{\delta}
.
Then, from (
1.43
),
we have

\mathbb{E}[Y_{\delta,N}^{2}(1)]=\sigma_{\delta,N}\sim_{\delta}\log(N+1).

Next, we recall the Boué-Dupuis variational formula.
Let
\mathbb{H}_{a}
denote the collection of drifts,
which are progressively measurable processes
belonging to

L^{2}([0,1];L^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{T}))
,
\mathbb{P}
-almost surely.
We now state the Boué-Dupuis variational formula
[
11
,
91
]
.
See, in particular, Theorem 7 in
[
91
]
.

Lemma 3.13
.

Given
0<\delta\leq\infty
,
let
Y_{\delta}
be as in (
3.72
).
Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Suppose that
F:C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})\to\mathbb{R}

is measurable such that
\mathbb{E}\big[|F(Y_{\delta,N}(1))|^{p}\big]<\infty

and
\mathbb{E}\big[|e^{-F(Y_{\delta,N}(1))}|^{q}\big]<\infty
for some
1<p,q<\infty
with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle-\log\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{-F(Y_{\delta,N}(1))}\Big]=\inf_{\theta\in\mathbb{H}_{a}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[F(Y_{\delta,N}(1)+\mathbf{P}_{N}I_{\delta}(\theta)(1))+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\|\theta(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}dt\bigg],

(3.73)

where
I_{\delta}(\theta)
is defined by

\displaystyle I_{\delta}(\theta)(t)=\int_{0}^{t}(\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\theta(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}

and the expectation
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}

is an
expectation with respect to the underlying probability measure
\mathbb{P}
.

Remark 3.14
.

(i)
As far as the proof of Proposition
3.12
is concerned,
we only need to work with
Y_{\delta,N}
evaluated at time
t=1
.
As such, we could have stated Lemma
3.13

with
X_{\delta,N}
in place of
Y_{\delta,N}(1)
, thus allowing
us to avoid introducing
W(t)
in (
3.71
) and
Y_{\delta}(t)
in (
3.72
).
We, however, did not do so since the natural setting
of the Boué-Dupuis formula is as stated above.
For example, (
3.73
) allows us to choose a
Y_{\delta}
-dependent drift
\theta
,
which is crucial in showing non-normalizability of the focusing Gibbs measures

\rho_{\delta}
. See
[
71
]
.

(ii) In view of the discussion above,
in order to prove Proposition
3.12
,
it is possible to work with
a slightly different and weaker variational formula
stated in
[
39
, Proposition 4.4]
,
where an expectation is taken
with respect to a shifted measure.

In the following, we
prove Proposition
3.12

by applying
Lemma
3.13

to
\mathcal{G}^{K}_{\delta,N}(u)
in (
3.67
).
Before proceeding to the proof of Proposition
3.12
,
let us state a preliminary lemma
on the pathwise regularity bounds of

Y_{\delta,N}(1)
and
I_{\delta}(\theta)(1)
.

Lemma 3.15
.

(i)

Let
\varepsilon>0
and fix finite
p\geq 1
.
Then, given any
0<\delta\leq\infty
,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\Big[\|Y_{\delta,N}(1)&\|_{W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}^{p}+\|\mathcal{W}(Y_{\delta,N}^{2}(1))\|_{W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}^{p}\\
&+\big\|\mathcal{W}(Y_{\delta,N}^{3}(1))\big\|_{W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}^{p}\Big]\leq C_{\varepsilon,p,\delta}<\infty,\end{split}

(3.74)

uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Furthermore,
by restricting our attention to
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
,
we can choose the constant
C_{\varepsilon,p,\delta}
in (
3.74
)
to be independent of
\delta
.

(ii)

Let
0<\delta\leq\infty
.
For any
\theta\in\mathbb{H}_{a}
, we have

\displaystyle\|I_{\delta}(\theta)(1)\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}\leq C_{\delta}\int_{0}^{1}\|\theta(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}dt,

(3.75)

where the constant
C_{\delta}>0
can be chosen
to be independent of
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.

Proof.

By noting that
\mathcal{L}(Y_{\delta,N}(1))=\mathcal{L}(X_{\delta,N})
,
we see that
Part (i) follows from
Proposition
3.4
.
As for the bound (
3.75
),
it follows from Minkowski’s and Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequalities and the lower bound
(
2.2
)
of
K_{\delta}(n)
that

\displaystyle\|I_{\delta}(\theta)(1)\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\langle\nabla\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\int_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\theta(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}\bigg\|_{L^{2}_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\delta}\int_{0}^{1}\|\theta(t)\|_{L^{2}}dt\leq C_{\delta}\bigg(\int_{0}^{1}\|\theta(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

When
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
,
the lower bound
(
2.3
) allows us to choose the constant
C_{\delta}

to be independent of
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
∎

Fix
0<\delta\leq\infty
and finite
p\geq 1
.
We
first prove the bound (
3.69
).
In view of the Boué-Dupuis formula (Lemma
3.13
),
it suffices to establish a lower bound on

\mathcal{M}_{\delta,N}(\theta)=\mathbb{E}\bigg[p\mathcal{R}_{\delta,N}(Y_{\delta}(1)+I_{\delta}(\theta)(1))+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\|\theta(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}dt\bigg],

(3.76)

uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\theta\in\mathbb{H}_{a}
.
We set

Y_{\delta,N}=\mathbf{P}_{N}Y_{\delta}=\mathbf{P}_{N}Y_{\delta}(1)\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\Theta_{\delta,N}=\mathbf{P}_{N}\Theta_{\delta}=\mathbf{P}_{N}I_{\delta}(\theta)(1).

From (
3.66
) and (
2.18
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{R}_{\delta,N}(Y_{\delta}+\Theta_{\delta})&=\frac{1}{3}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(Y_{\delta,N}^{3})dx+\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(Y_{\delta,N}^{2})\Theta_{\delta,N}dx+\int_{\mathbb{T}}Y_{\delta,N}\Theta_{\delta,N}^{2}dx\\
&\hphantom{X}+\frac{1}{3}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Theta_{\delta,N}^{3}dx+A\bigg\{\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big(\mathcal{W}(Y_{\delta,N}^{2})+2Y_{\delta,N}\Theta_{\delta,N}+\Theta_{\delta,N}^{2}\Big)dx\bigg\}^{2},\end{split}

(3.77)

where the first term on the right-hand side vanishes under the expectation.
Hence, from (
3.76
) and (
3.77
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{\delta,N}(\theta)&=\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\,p\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(Y_{\delta,N}^{2})\Theta_{\delta,N}dx+p\int_{\mathbb{T}}Y_{\delta,N}\Theta_{\delta,N}^{2}dx+\frac{p}{3}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Theta_{\delta,N}^{3}dx\\
&\hphantom{XXX}+Ap\bigg\{\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big(\mathcal{W}(Y_{\delta,N}^{2})+2Y_{\delta,N}\Theta_{\delta,N}+\Theta_{\delta,N}^{2}\Big)dx\bigg\}^{2}\\
&\hphantom{XXX}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\|\theta(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}dt\Bigg].\end{split}

(3.78)

We now recall the following lemma from
[
71
, Lemma 4.1]
,
where the
p=1
case was treated.
See also Lemma 5.8 in
[
66
]
.

Lemma 3.16
.

(i)

There exist small
\varepsilon>0
and a constant
c=c(p)>0

and
C_{0}>0
such that

\displaystyle p\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(Y_{\delta,N}^{2})\Theta_{\delta,N}dx\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq c\|\mathcal{W}(Y_{\delta,N}^{2})\|_{W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}^{2}+\frac{1}{100}\|\Theta_{\delta,N}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2},

\displaystyle p\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{T}}Y_{\delta,N}\Theta_{\delta,N}^{2}dx\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq c\|Y_{\delta,N}\|_{W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}^{6}+\frac{1}{100}\Big(\|\Theta_{\delta,N}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}+\|\Theta_{\delta,N}\|_{L^{2}}^{4}\Big),

\displaystyle\frac{p}{3}\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Theta_{\delta,N}^{3}dx\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{100}\|\Theta_{\delta,N}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}+C_{0}p^{2}\|\Theta_{\delta,N}\|_{L^{2}}^{4},

uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
0<\delta\leq\infty
.

(ii)
Let
A>0
. Given any small
\varepsilon>0
,
there exists
c=c(\varepsilon,p,A)>0
such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}Ap\bigg\{&\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big(\mathcal{W}(Y_{\delta,N}^{2})+2Y_{\delta,N}\Theta_{\delta,N}+\Theta_{\delta,N}^{2}\Big)dx\bigg\}^{2}\\
&\geq\frac{Ap}{4}\|\Theta_{\delta,N}\|_{L^{2}}^{4}-\frac{1}{100}\|\Theta_{\delta,N}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}-c\bigg\{\|Y_{\delta,N}\|_{W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}^{c}+\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(Y_{\delta,N}^{2})dx\bigg)^{2}\bigg\},\end{split}

(3.79)

uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}

and
0<\delta\leq\infty
.

As in
[
71
]
, we establish a pathwise lower bound on
\mathcal{M}_{\delta,N}(\theta)
in (
3.78
),
uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\theta\in\mathbb{H}_{a}
,
by making use of the
positive terms:

\mathcal{U}_{\delta,N}(\theta)=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{Ap}{4}\|\Theta_{\delta,N}\|_{L^{2}}^{4}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\|\theta(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}dt\bigg].

(3.80)

coming from (
3.78
) and (
3.79
).
From (
3.78
) and (
3.80
) together with Lemmas
3.16
and
3.15
, we obtain

\displaystyle\inf_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\inf_{\theta\in\mathbb{H}_{a}}\mathcal{M}_{\delta,N}(\theta)\geq\inf_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\inf_{\theta\in\mathbb{H}_{a}}\Big\{-C_{p,\delta,A}+\frac{1}{10}\mathcal{U}_{\delta,N}(\theta)\Big\}\geq-C_{p,\delta,A}>-\infty,

(3.81)

provided that
A=A(p)\gg 1
is sufficiently large.
Hence, the uniform (in
N
) bound (
3.69
)
follows from
Lemma
3.13
with (
3.67
) and (
3.81
).

Next, we restrict our attention to
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
In this case,
the constant
C_{\varepsilon,p,\delta}
in (
3.74
) of Lemma
3.15

is independent of
\delta

and, as a result, we see that the constant
C_{p,\delta,A}
in (
3.81
)
is also independent of
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
Therefore, the second bound (
3.70
) follows from
Lemma
3.13
with (
3.67
) and (
3.81
).
This concludes the proof of Proposition
3.12

and hence of Theorem
1.3
when
k=2
.

4.
Gibbs measures in the shallow-water regime

In this section, we present the proof of Theorem
1.5
.
Namely, we go over the construction and convergence in the shallow-water limit (
\delta\to 0
)
of the Gibbs measure
\widetilde{\rho}_{\delta}
associated
with the scaled gILW equation (
1.17
).
For each fixed
0<\delta<\infty
,
the scaling transformation (
1.16
)
simply introduces a constant factor, depending on
\delta
.
Hence, the regularity properties
of the support of the base Gaussian measures

\mu_{\delta}
in (
1.22
)
for the unscaled problem
and

\widetilde{\mu}_{\delta}
in (
1.24
)
for the scaled problem
are the same
for each fixed
0<\delta<\infty
,
and thus
we can repeat the argument in Section
3

to construct the Gibbs measure
\widetilde{\rho}_{\delta}
supported on
H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})\setminus L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
,

\varepsilon>0
,
yielding Theorem
1.5
(i)
for
each fixed

0<\delta<\infty
.
The main difference in this shallow-water regime
appears in
establishing uniform (in
\delta
) bounds
and convergence as
\delta\to 0
.
This is
due to the singularity of the base Gaussian measures

\widetilde{\mu}_{\delta}
,
0<\delta<\infty
, supported on
H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})\setminus L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
,
and
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}
in (
1.29
)
supported on
H^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})\setminus H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{T})
;
see Proposition
4.1
.

In Subsection
4.1
,
we first study the singularity and convergence properties
of the base Gaussian measures.
Then, we briefly go over the construction
and convergence of the Gibbs measure
\widetilde{\rho}_{\delta}

for the defocusing case (
2\mathbb{N}+1
)
in Subsections
4.2

and

4.3
.
In Subsection
4.4
,
we discuss the variational approach to treat the
k=2
case.

4.1.
Singularity of the base Gaussian measures

Given
0<\delta<\infty
,
let

\widetilde{\mu}_{\delta}
be as in (
1.24
)
and let

\widetilde{\mu}_{0}
be as in (
1.29
).
Then, a typical element
under
\widetilde{\mu}_{\delta}

(and under
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}
, respectively)
is given by
the Gaussian Fourier series

\widetilde{X}_{\delta}
in (
1.51
)
(and
by
X_{\text{\rm KdV}}
in (
1.30
), respectively).
Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
set

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}_{\delta,N}=\mathbf{P}_{N}\widetilde{X}_{\delta,N}\qquad\text{and}\qquad X_{\text{\rm KdV},N}=\mathbf{P}_{N}X_{\text{\rm KdV}}.

(4.1)

Then, in view of (
1.53
),
we see that, for each
0<\delta<\infty
,
\widetilde{X}_{\delta,N}

converges in
L^{p}(\Omega)
for any finite
p\geq 1
and almost surely to
the limit
\widetilde{X}_{\delta}
in
H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})\setminus L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
,
\varepsilon>0
,
as
N\to\infty
.
On the other hand, it is well known
[
13
,
69
]
that
X_{\text{\rm KdV},N}

converges, in
L^{p}(\Omega)
and almost surely, to
the limit
X_{\text{\rm KdV}}
in
H^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})\setminus H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{T})
,
\varepsilon>0
,
as
N\to\infty
.

Proposition 4.1
.

(i)

Given any
\varepsilon>0

and finite
p\geq 1
,

\widetilde{X}_{\delta}
converges to
X_{\text{\rm KdV}}
in
L^{p}(\Omega;H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}))

and almost surely in
H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})
,
as
\delta\to 0
. In particular, the Gaussian measure
\widetilde{\mu}_{\delta}

converges weakly to the Gaussian measure
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}
, as
\delta\to 0
.

(ii)

Let
\varepsilon>0
. Then,
for any
0<\delta<\infty
, the Gaussian measures
\widetilde{\mu}_{\delta}
and
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}
are singular
as probability measures
H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})
.

In Section
3
,
the convergence in total variation of
\mu_{\delta}
to
\mu_{\infty}
played
an essential role in establishing
the convergence in total variation of
\rho_{\delta}
to
\rho_{\text{\rm BO}}
.
Proposition
4.1

only provides weak convergence of the
base Gaussian measures
\widetilde{\mu}_{\delta}

to
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}
,
and
the singularity between the base Gaussian measures suggests that
we do not expect any stronger mode of convergence
(such as convergence in total variation).
As a result,
we only expect weak convergence of the associated Gibbs measures

\widetilde{\rho}_{\delta}
to
\rho_{\text{\rm KdV}}
in (
1.57
)
in the shallow-water limit (
\delta\to 0
).

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
.
From
(
1.30
),
(
1.51
), and Lemma
2.7
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\widetilde{X}_{\delta}-X_{\text{\rm KdV}}\|_{L^{p}_{\omega}H_{x}^{-\varepsilon}}\ &\lesssim_{p}\|\langle\nabla\rangle^{-\varepsilon}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta}-X_{\text{\rm KdV}})(x)\|_{L^{2}_{x}L^{2}_{\omega}}\\
&\sim\Bigg(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ast}}\frac{1}{\langle n\rangle^{2\varepsilon}}\bigg(\frac{1}{L_{\delta}^{\frac{1}{2}}(n)}-\frac{1}{|n|}\bigg)^{2}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.\end{split}

(4.2)

It follows from (
2.8
) in
Lemma
2.3

that
the summand is bounded by
\langle n\rangle^{-1-2\varepsilon}

uniformly in
0<\delta\lesssim 1
,
which is summable in
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.
Moreover,
from Lemma
2.3
(ii),
we see that, for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
, the summand tends to 0
as
\delta\to 0
.
Hence, by the dominated convergence theorem,
we conclude that
\widetilde{X}_{\delta}

converges to
X_{\text{\rm KdV}}
in
L^{p}(\Omega;H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}))
.
As for almost sure convergence,
we repeat a computation analogous to (
4.2
)
but with (
2.19
) in place of
\mathbb{E}[|g_{n}|^{2}]\sim 1
.
We omit details.
See
(
3.6
) for an analogous argument in the unscaled case.

Next we prove part (ii) by using Lemma
3.2
.
From (
1.51
) and (
1.30
), we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}_{\delta}(\omega)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\bigg(\frac{\Re g_{n}}{\pi L_{\delta}^{\frac{1}{2}}(n)}\cos(nx)-\frac{\Im g_{n}}{\pi L_{\delta}^{\frac{1}{2}}(n)}\sin(nx)\bigg)

for
0\leq\delta<\infty

with the understanding that
\widetilde{X}_{0}=X_{\text{\rm KdV}}
and
L_{0}(n)=n^{2}
.
For
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
, set

A_{n}=\frac{\Re g_{n}}{\pi L_{\delta}^{\frac{1}{2}}(n)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad A_{-n}=-\frac{\Im g_{n}}{\pi L_{\delta}^{\frac{1}{2}}(n)},

and

B_{n}=\frac{\Re g_{n}}{\pi|n|}\qquad\text{and}\qquad B_{-n}=-\frac{\Im g_{n}}{\pi|n|}.

with
a_{\pm n}=\mathbb{E}[A_{\pm n}^{2}]=\frac{1}{\pi L_{\delta}(n)}
and
b_{\pm n}=\mathbb{E}[B_{\pm n}^{2}]=\frac{1}{\pi n^{2}}
.
Then, from
Lemma
2.3
(iv), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\Big(\frac{b_{n}}{a_{n}}-1\Big)^{2}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ast}}\frac{(n^{2}-L_{\delta}(n))^{2}}{n^{4}}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ast}}h^{2}(n,\delta)=\infty

for any
\delta>0
,
where
h(n,\delta)
is as in (
2.9
).
Therefore,
we conclude from Kakutani’s theorem (Lemma
3.2
)
that, for any
0<\delta<\infty
,
the Gaussian measures
\widetilde{\mu}_{\delta}

and
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}
are mutually singular.
∎

4.2.
Construction of the Gibbs measures for the defocusing scaled gILW equation

In this subsection, we briefly go over the construction
of the Gibbs measure
\widetilde{\rho}_{\delta}
,
0<\delta<\infty
,
for the scaled gILW equation (
1.17
)
in the defocusing case
k\in 2\mathbb{N}+1
.
(Theorem
1.5
(i)).
We treat the
k=2
case
in Subsection
4.4
.

Fix the depth parameter
0<\delta<\infty
.
Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\widetilde{X}_{\delta,N}=\mathbf{P}_{N}\widetilde{X}_{\delta}
,
where
\widetilde{X}_{\delta}
is defined in (
1.51
).
Given
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
let

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta,N}^{k})=H_{k}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta,N};\widetilde{\sigma}_{\delta,N})

(4.3)

denote the Wick power defined in (
1.55
),
21
21

21

As in Section
3
,
we freely interchange
the representations in terms of
\widetilde{X}_{\delta}

and in terms of
v
distributed by
\widetilde{\mu}_{\delta}
,
when there is no confusion.

where
\widetilde{\sigma}_{\delta,N}
is as in (
1.54
).
Then, the truncated Gibbs measure
\widetilde{\rho}_{\delta,N}
in (
1.56
)
can be written as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}_{\delta,N}(A)

\displaystyle=Z_{\delta,N}^{-1}\int_{H^{-\varepsilon}}\mathbf{1}_{\{v\in A\}}e^{-\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(v_{N}^{k+1})dx}d\widetilde{\mu}_{\delta}(v)

\displaystyle=Z_{\delta,N}^{-1}\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{1}_{\{\widetilde{X}_{\delta}(\omega)\in A\}}e^{-\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta,N}^{k+1}(\omega))dx}d\mathbb{P}(\omega),

where
v_{N}=\mathbf{P}_{N}v
.
By repeating the proof of Proposition
3.4

in the unscaled setting,
we obtain the following result.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
0<\delta<\infty
.
Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let

\mathcal{W}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta,N}^{k})
be as in (
4.3
).
Then, given any finite
p\geq 1
,
the sequence
\{\mathcal{W}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta,N}^{k})\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

is Cauchy in
L^{p}(\Omega;W^{s,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))
,
s<0
,
thus converging to a limit denoted by

\mathcal{W}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta}^{k})
.
This convergence of
\mathcal{W}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta,N}^{k})

to
\mathcal{W}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta}^{k})
also holds almost surely in
W^{s,\infty}(\mathbb{T})
.
Furthermore, given any finite
p\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{0<\delta\leq 1}\big\|\|\mathcal{W}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta,N}^{k})\|_{W^{s,\infty}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}<\infty

and

\displaystyle\sup_{0<\delta\leq 1}\big\|\|\mathcal{W}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta,M}^{k})-\mathcal{W}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta,N}^{k})\|_{W^{s,\infty}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\longrightarrow 0

for any
M\geq N
, tending to
\infty
.
In particular, the rate of convergence is uniform in
0<\delta\leq 1
.

As a corollary, the following two statements hold.

(i)

Let

0<\delta<\infty
.
Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
\widetilde{R}_{\delta,N}(v)=\widetilde{R}_{\delta,N}(v;k+1)
denote the truncated potential energy defined by

\displaystyle\widetilde{R}_{\delta,N}(v):=\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}((\mathbf{P}_{N}v)^{k+1})dx=\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}H_{k+1}\big(\mathbf{P}_{N}v;\widetilde{\sigma}_{\delta,N}\big)dx,

(4.4)

where
\widetilde{\sigma}_{\delta,N}
is as in (
1.54
).
Then, given any finite
p\geq 1
,
the sequence

\{\widetilde{R}_{\delta,N}(v)\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

converges to the limit
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{R}_{\delta}(v)=\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(v^{k+1})dx=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}((\mathbf{P}_{N}v)^{k+1})dx

(4.5)

in
L^{p}(d\mu_{\delta})
, as
N\to\infty
.
Furthermore,
there exists
\theta>0
such that given any finite
p\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}}\sup_{0<\delta\leq 1}\|\widetilde{R}_{\delta,N}(v)\|_{L^{p}(d\widetilde{\mu}_{\delta})}<\infty,

(4.6)

with
\widetilde{R}_{\delta,\infty}(v)=\widetilde{R}_{\delta}(v)
,
and

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{R}_{\delta,M}(v)-\widetilde{R}_{\delta,N}(v)\|_{L^{p}(d\widetilde{\mu}_{\delta})}\leq\frac{C_{k,\delta}\,p^{\frac{k+1}{2}}}{N^{\theta}}

(4.7)

for
any
M\geq N\geq 1
.
For
0<\delta\leq 1
,
we can choose the constant
C_{k,\delta}
in (
4.7
) to be independent of
\delta

and hence the rate of convergence
of
\widetilde{R}_{\delta,N}(v)
to the limit
\widetilde{R}_{\delta}(v)
is uniform in
0<\delta\leq 1
.

(ii)

Let

0<\delta<\infty
.
Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
\widetilde{F}_{N}(u)=\widetilde{F}_{N}(u;k)
be the truncated renormalized nonlinearity
in (
1.72
) given
by

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}_{N}(v):=\partial_{x}\mathbf{P}_{N}\mathcal{W}((\mathbf{P}_{N}v)^{k})=\partial_{x}\mathbf{P}_{N}H_{k}(\mathbf{P}_{N}v;\widetilde{\sigma}_{\delta,N}),

where
\widetilde{\sigma}_{\delta,N}
is as in (
1.54
).
Then, given any finite
p\geq 1
,
the sequence
\{\widetilde{F}_{N}(v)\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

is Cauchy in
L^{p}(d\widetilde{\mu}_{\delta};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
,
s<-1
,
thus converging to a limit denoted by

\widetilde{F}(v)=\partial_{x}\mathcal{W}(v^{k})
.
Furthermore, given any finite
p\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{0<\delta\leq 1}\big\|\|\widetilde{F}_{N}(v)\|_{H^{s}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}(d\widetilde{\mu}_{\delta})}<\infty

and

\displaystyle\sup_{0<\delta\leq 1}\big\|\|\widetilde{F}_{M}(v)-\widetilde{F}_{N}(v)\|_{H^{s}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}(d\widetilde{\mu}_{\delta})}\longrightarrow 0

for any
M\geq N
, tending to
\infty
.
In particular, the rate of convergence
of
\widetilde{F}_{N}(v)
to the limit
\widetilde{F}(v)

is uniform in
0<\delta\leq 1
.

Proof.

Proposition
4.2

follows from a straightforward modification
of the proof of Proposition
3.4
.
The only notable difference is that instead of using the bounds
(
2.2
) and (
2.3
)
for
K_{\delta}(n)
, we need to use the bounds (
2.7
) and (
2.8
) for
L_{\delta}(n)
.
We omit details.
∎

Given
0<\delta<\infty

and
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
we define
\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})=\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta};k+1)
by

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})=e^{-\widetilde{R}_{\delta,N}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})}=e^{-\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta,N}^{k+1})dx},

where
\widetilde{R}_{\delta,N}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})=\widetilde{R}_{\delta,N}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta};k+1)

is the truncated potential energy defined
in (
4.4
).
Then, a slight modification of the proof of Proposition
3.6

yields the following proposition.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
k\in 2\mathbb{N}+1

and fix finite
p\geq 1
.
Given any
0<\delta<\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\|\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\|\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}(v)\|_{L^{p}(d\widetilde{\mu}_{\delta})}\leq C_{p,k,\delta}<\infty.

In addition, the following uniform bound holds for
0<\delta\leq 1
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{0<\delta\leq 1}\|\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}&=\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{0<\delta\leq 1}\|\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}(v)\|_{L^{p}(d\widetilde{\mu}_{\delta})}\\
&\leq C_{p,k}<\infty.\end{split}

(4.8)

Define
\widetilde{G}_{\delta}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})
by

\widetilde{G}_{\delta}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})=e^{-\widetilde{R}_{\delta}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})}

with
\widetilde{R}_{\delta}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})
as in (
4.5
).
Then,

\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})
converges to
\widetilde{G}_{\delta}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})

in
L^{p}(\Omega)
.
Namely,
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\|\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})-\widetilde{G}_{\delta}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=0.

Furthermore,
the convergence is uniform in
0<\delta\leq 1
:

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{0<\delta\leq 1}\|\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})-\widetilde{G}_{\delta}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=0.

(4.9)

As a consequence, the uniform bounds (
4.8
)
and (
4.9
) hold even if we replace the supremum in
N\in\mathbb{N}

by the supremum in
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
.

Theorem
1.5
(i)
follows as a direct corollary to
Proposition
4.3
,
allowing us to define the limiting
Gibbs measure
\widetilde{\rho}_{\delta}
in (
1.61
).
See the discussion right after Proposition
3.6
.

For
0<\delta<\infty
,
the Gibbs measure
\widetilde{\rho}_{\delta}
is equivalent to the base Gaussian measure
\widetilde{\mu}_{\delta}
.
Similarly, the Gibbs measure
\rho_{\text{\rm KdV}}
in (
1.57
)
equivalent to the base Gaussian measure
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}
.
Recalling from
Proposition
4.1
that the base Gaussian measures
\widetilde{\mu}_{\delta}
,
0<\delta<\infty
,
and
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}
are mutually singular,
we conclude that
the Gibbs measures
\widetilde{\rho}_{\delta}
in (
1.61
)
and
\rho_{\text{\rm KdV}}
in (
1.57
)
are mutually singular.
This proves the first claim in
Theorem
1.5
(ii).

Proof of Proposition
4.3
.

From
(
3.37
) with (
1.54
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} -\widetilde{R}_{\delta,N}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})&=-\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}H_{k+1}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta,N};\widetilde{\sigma}_{\delta,N})dx\\
&\leq\frac{2\pi}{k+1}\widetilde{\sigma}_{\delta,N}^{\frac{k+1}{2}}a_{k+1}\leq\widetilde{A}_{k,\delta}(\log(N+1))^{\frac{k+1}{2}}\end{aligned}

(4.10)

for some
\widetilde{A}_{k,\delta}>0
,
uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then, we can simply repeat the proof of Proposition
3.6
,
using Proposition
4.2
in place of Proposition
3.4
.

For
0<\delta\leq 1
, it follows from (
1.54
)
and Lemma
2.3
that the constant
\widetilde{A}_{k,\delta}

in (
4.10
) can be chosen to be independent of
0<\delta\leq 1
.
Similarly,
by restricting our attention to
0<\delta\leq 1
,
we can choose the constant

c_{k,\delta}
in an analogue of (
3.39
)
in the current setting to be
independent of
0<\delta\leq 1

since the constant
C_{k,\delta}
in (
4.7
) is independent of

0<\delta\leq 1
.
Moreover, in applying Lemma
2.9

in Step 2 of the proof of Proposition
3.6
,
we need the uniform bound (
4.6
), replacing
(
3.16
).
This observation yields the uniform bounds (
4.8
)
and (
4.9
).
∎

Remark 4.4
.

Given
N
, define
\sigma_{\text{\rm KdV},N}
by

\displaystyle\sigma_{\text{\rm KdV},N}=\mathbb{E}\big[X_{\text{\rm KdV},N}^{2}(x)\big]=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\sum_{0<|n|\leq N}\frac{2\pi}{n^{2}},

(4.11)

which is uniformly bounded in
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Here,
X_{\text{\rm KdV},N}
is as in (
4.1
).
We then extend the definition of
L_{\delta}(n)
and
\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}

to the
\delta=0
case by setting
L_{0}(n)=n^{2}
and

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{0,N}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}})=e^{-\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(X_{\text{\rm KdV},N}^{k+1})dx},

(4.12)

where

\mathcal{W}(X_{\text{\rm KdV},N}^{k+1})=H_{k+1}(X_{\text{\rm KdV},N};\sigma_{\text{\rm KdV},N})
.
We also set

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{0}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}})=e^{-\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}}^{k+1})dx},

(4.13)

where

\mathcal{W}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}}^{k+1})=H_{k+1}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}};\sigma_{\text{\rm KdV}})

as in (
1.59
).
Then, by setting

\widetilde{X}_{0}=X_{\text{\rm KdV}}
,
Proposition
4.3

extends to
\delta=0
.
In particular, the uniform bounds (
4.8
)
and (
4.9
) hold for
0\leq\delta\leq 1
.

4.3.
Convergence of the Gibbs measures in the shallow-water limit

It remains to prove that
the Gibbs measure
\widetilde{\rho}_{\delta}
converges weakly to
\rho_{\text{\rm KdV}}

as
\delta\to 0
. We first state an analogue of Lemma
3.8
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
k\in 2\mathbb{N}+1
and
1\leq p<\infty
. Then, given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\|\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})-\widetilde{G}_{0,N}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=0.

As a corollary, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to\infty}\|\widetilde{G}_{\delta}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})-\widetilde{G}_{0}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=0.

In particular, the partition function

Z_{\delta}
of the Gibbs measure
\widetilde{\rho}_{\delta}
in (
1.61
)
converges to the partition function
Z_{\text{\rm KdV}}=Z_{0}

of the Gibbs measure
\rho_{\text{\rm KdV}}=\widetilde{\rho}_{0}

in (
1.57
), as
\delta\to 0
.

Proof.

From Lemma
2.3
,
we see that
\widetilde{\sigma}_{\delta,N}
in (
1.54
)
converges to
\sigma_{\text{\rm KdV},N}
in (
4.11
) as
\delta\to 0
.
With this observation, we can simply repeat the proof of Lemma
3.8
.
We omit details.
∎

We are now ready to prove weak convergence of
\widetilde{\rho}_{\delta}
to
\rho_{\text{\rm KdV}}

in the shallow-water limit (
\delta\to 0
).
Fix small
\varepsilon>0
.
Let
A
be any Borel subset of
H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})
with

\widetilde{\mu}_{0}(\partial A)=0
,
where
\partial A
denotes the boundary of the set
A
.
Our goal is to show that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}_{\delta}(A)-\rho_{\text{\rm KdV}}(A)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 0

(4.14)

as
\delta\to 0
,
which, together with the portmanteau theorem, yields the desired weak convergence.

By the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\widetilde{\rho}_{\delta}(A)-\rho_{\text{\rm KdV}}(A)|&\leq|\widetilde{\rho}_{\delta}(A)-\widetilde{\rho}_{\delta,N}(A)|\\
&\quad+|\widetilde{\rho}_{\delta,N}(A)-\rho_{\text{\rm KdV},N}(A)|+|\rho_{\text{\rm KdV},N}(A)-\rho_{\text{\rm KdV}}(A)|,\end{split}

(4.15)

where
\rho_{\text{\rm KdV},N}
denotes the truncated Gibbs measure for
\delta=0

given by

\displaystyle\rho_{\text{\rm KdV},N}(A)

\displaystyle=Z_{0,N}^{-1}\int_{H^{-\varepsilon}}\mathbf{1}_{\{v\in A\}}e^{-\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(v_{N}^{k+1})dx}d\widetilde{\mu}_{0}(v)

\displaystyle=Z_{0,N}^{-1}\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{\text{\rm KdV}}(\omega)\in A\}}\widetilde{G}_{0,N}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}})d\mathbb{P}(\omega)

for any measurable set
A\subset H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})
.
From Proposition
4.3

and Remark
4.4
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{0\leq\delta\leq 1}|\widetilde{\rho}_{\delta}(A)-\widetilde{\rho}_{\delta,N}(A)|\\
&\quad=\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{0\leq\delta\leq 1}\|\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})-\widetilde{G}_{\delta}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\\
&\quad=0,\end{split}

(4.16)

with the identification
\widetilde{X}_{0}=X_{\text{\rm KdV}}
,

\widetilde{\rho}_{0}=\rho_{\text{\rm KdV}}
,
and
\widetilde{\rho}_{0,N}=\rho_{\text{\rm KdV},N}
,
where
\widetilde{G}_{0,N}(\widetilde{X}_{0})

and
\widetilde{G}_{0}(\widetilde{X}_{0})
are as in (
4.12
) and (
4.13
), respectively.
Hence, in view of (
4.14
), (
4.15
),
and (
4.16
), it suffices to prove

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lim_{\delta\to 0}|\widetilde{\rho}_{\delta,N}(A)-\rho_{\text{\rm KdV},N}(A)|\\
&\quad=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\Big|Z_{\delta,N}^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})\mathbf{1}_{A}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})]-Z_{0,N}^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{G}_{0,N}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}})\mathbf{1}_{A}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}})]\Big|\\
&\quad=0\end{split}

(4.17)

for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

First,
note that it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})\mathbf{1}_{A}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})]-\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{G}_{0,N}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}})\mathbf{1}_{A}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}})]\longrightarrow 0

(4.18)

as
\delta\to 0

since, by taking
A=H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})
,
(
4.18
) implies
Z_{\delta,N}\to Z_{0,N}
as
\delta\to 0
.

By the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big|&\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})\mathbf{1}_{A}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})]-\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{G}_{0,N}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}})\mathbf{1}_{A}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}})]\big|\\
&\leq\mathbb{E}\big[|\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})-\widetilde{G}_{0,N}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}})|\big]\\
&\quad+\mathbb{E}\big[\widetilde{G}_{0,N}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}})|\mathbf{1}_{A}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})-\mathbf{1}_{A}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}})|\big].\end{split}

(4.19)

From Lemma
4.5
,
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[|\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})-\widetilde{G}_{0,N}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}})|\big]\longrightarrow 0,

(4.20)

as
\delta\to\infty
.
As for the second term
on the right-hand side of (
4.19
),
we first note that

\sigma_{\text{\rm KdV},N}
defined in (
4.11
) is uniformly bounded in
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then, together with (
3.37
) and (
4.12
),
we conclude that

\displaystyle 0<\widetilde{G}_{0,N}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}}(\omega))\lesssim 1,

(4.21)

uniformly in
\omega\in\Omega
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Hence, from (
4.21
) and
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}(\partial A)=0

(which implies
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\partial A}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}})]=0
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}&\big[\widetilde{G}_{0,N}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}})|\mathbf{1}_{A}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})-\mathbf{1}_{A}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}})|\big]\\
&\lesssim\mathbb{E}\big[|\mathbf{1}_{A}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})-\mathbf{1}_{A}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}})|\big]\\
&=\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbf{1}_{\text{int}A}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}})\cdot|\mathbf{1}_{A}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})-\mathbf{1}_{A}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}})|\big]\\
&\quad+\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbf{1}_{\text{int}A^{c}}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}})\cdot|\mathbf{1}_{A}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})-\mathbf{1}_{A}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}})|\big],\end{split}

(4.22)

where
\text{int}A
denotes the interior of
A

given by
\text{int}A=A\setminus\partial A
.
From
Proposition
4.1
(i)
and the openness of
\text{int}A
and
\text{int}A^{c}
,
the integrands of the terms on the right-hand side
of (
4.22
) tend to
0
as
\delta\to 0
.
Hence, by the bounded convergence theorem,
we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\widetilde{G}_{0,N}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}})|\mathbf{1}_{A}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})-\mathbf{1}_{A}(X_{\text{\rm KdV}})|\big]\longrightarrow 0,

(4.23)

as
\delta\to 0
.
Therefore,
putting (
4.19
), (
4.20
),
and (
4.23
) together,
we conclude (
4.18
),
which in turn implies (
4.17
).
Finally, from (
4.15
), (
4.16
),
and (
4.17
),
we conclude (
4.14
),
namely, weak convergence of
\widetilde{\rho}_{\delta}
to
\rho_{\text{\rm KdV}}

as
\delta\to 0
.
This concludes the proof of Theorem
1.5

when
k\in 2\mathbb{N}+1
.

4.4.
Gibbs measures for the scaled ILW equation: variational approach

We conclude this section by briefly going over the proof of Theorem
1.5

when
k=2
, based on the variational approach as in Subsection
3.4
.
The major part of the argument follows
exactly as in Subsection
3.4

and thus we only describe necessary definitions and steps.

Fix
K>0
in the remaining part of this section.
Given
0\leq\delta<\infty
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
define
the truncated density
\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}^{K}(v)
by

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}^{K}(v)

\displaystyle=\chi_{K}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(v_{N}^{2})dx\bigg)e^{-\frac{1}{3}\int_{\mathbb{T}}v_{N}^{3}dx}

\displaystyle=\chi_{K}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{T}}H_{2}(v_{N};\widetilde{\sigma}_{\delta,N})dx\bigg)e^{-\frac{1}{3}\int_{\mathbb{T}}v_{N}^{3}dx},

where
v_{N}=\mathbf{P}_{N}v
,

\widetilde{\sigma}_{\delta,N}
is as in (
1.54
) when
0<\delta<\infty
,
and
\widetilde{\sigma}_{0,N}=\sigma_{\text{\rm KdV},N}
.
As in the unscaled case discussed in Subsection
3.4
,
Theorem
1.5
for
k=2
follows
once we prove the following uniform bounds.

Proposition 4.6
.

Fix finite
p\geq 1
and
K>0
.
Then, given any
0\leq\delta<\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\|\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}^{K}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\|\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}^{K}(v)\|_{L^{p}(d\widetilde{\mu}_{\delta})}\leq C_{p,\delta,K}<\infty.

In addition, the following uniform bound holds for
0\leq\delta\leq 1
:

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{0\leq\delta\leq 1}\|\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}^{K}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{0\leq\delta\leq 1}\|\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}^{K}(v)\|_{L^{p}(d\widetilde{\mu}_{\delta})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,K}<\infty.

Once we have Proposition
4.6
,
we can argue exactly as in Subsection
3.4

to conclude Theorem
1.5
.
In particular, (
3.64
) and (
3.65
)
provide a bound on the truncated density
\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}^{K}
, uniformly in
0\leq\delta\leq 1
,
replacing the defocusing bound (
4.10
).
We omit details.

In order to prove Proposition
4.6
,
we consider the truncated density
with a taming by a power of the Wick-ordered
L^{2}
-norm
as in Subsection
3.4
.
Given
0\leq\delta<\infty
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
, set

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\delta,N}(v)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}\int_{\mathbb{T}}v_{N}^{3}dx+A\,\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(v_{N}^{2})dx\bigg|^{2},

where

\mathcal{W}(v_{N}^{2})=\mathcal{W}_{\delta,N}(v_{N}^{2})=H_{2}(v_{N};\widetilde{\sigma}_{\delta,N})
.
Then, we also define the truncated density
with a taming by a power of the Wick-ordered
L^{2}
-norm:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta,N}^{K}(v)

\displaystyle=e^{-\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\delta,N}(v)}=e^{-\frac{1}{3}\int_{\mathbb{T}}v_{N}^{3}dx-A|\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{W}(v_{N}^{2})dx|^{2}}

for some suitable
A>0
.
Then, from (
3.68
), we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{\delta,N}^{K}(v)\leq C_{A,K}\cdot\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta,N}^{K}(v)

and, hence,
Proposition
4.6
follows once we prove the following
uniform bounds.

Proposition 4.7
.

Fix finite
p\geq 1
.
Then, there exists
A_{0}=A_{0}(p)>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta,N}^{K}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta,N}^{K}(v)\|_{L^{p}(d\widetilde{\mu}_{\delta})}\leq C_{p,\delta,K,A}<\infty

for any
0\leq\delta<\infty
,
K>0
,
and
A\geq A_{0}
.
In addition, the following uniform bound holds for
0\leq\delta\leq 1
:

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{0\leq\delta\leq 1}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta,N}^{K}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{0\leq\delta\leq 1}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta,N}^{K}(u)\|_{L^{p}(d\widetilde{\mu}_{\delta})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,K,A}<\infty

for any
K>0
and
A\geq A_{0}
.

In order to set up the variational formulation,
let us introduce some notations.
Define
\widetilde{Y}_{\delta}(t)

by

\displaystyle\widetilde{Y}_{\delta}(t)=\Big(\frac{3}{\delta}\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}\Big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}W(t),

(4.24)

where
W(t)
is as in (
3.71
)
and
\big(\frac{3}{\delta}\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}\big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
is the Fourier multiplier operator
with the multiplier
(L_{\delta}(n))^{-\frac{1}{2}}

with
L_{\delta}(n)
as in (
1.52
).
In view of (
1.51
), we have

\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{Y}_{\delta}(1))=\widetilde{\mu}_{\delta}
.
Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
we set
\widetilde{Y}_{\delta,N}=\mathbf{P}_{N}\widetilde{Y}_{\delta}
.
The variational formulation in the current problem is given by the following lemma.

Lemma 4.8
.

Given
0\leq\delta<\infty
,
let
\widetilde{Y}_{\delta}
be as in (
4.24
).
Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Suppose that
F:C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})\to\mathbb{R}

is measurable such that
\mathbb{E}\big[|F(\widetilde{Y}_{\delta,N}(1))|^{p}\big]<\infty

and
\mathbb{E}\big[|e^{-F(\widetilde{Y}_{\delta,N}(1))}|^{q}\big]<\infty
for some
1<p,q<\infty
with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle-\log\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{-F(\widetilde{Y}_{\delta,N}(1))}\Big]=\inf_{\theta\in\mathbb{H}_{a}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[F(\widetilde{Y}_{\delta,N}(1)+\mathbf{P}_{N}\widetilde{I}_{\delta}(\theta)(1))+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\|\theta(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}dt\bigg],

where
\widetilde{I}_{\delta}(\theta)
is defined by

\displaystyle\widetilde{I}_{\delta}(\theta)(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\big(\tfrac{3}{\delta}\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}\big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\theta(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}.

With Lemma
4.8
in hand,
we can proceed as in Subsection
3.4

to prove
Proposition
4.7

by using
Lemma
3.16

and the following lemma.

Lemma 4.9
.

(i)

Let
\varepsilon>0
and fix finite
p\geq 1
.
Then, given any
0\leq\delta<\infty
,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\Big[\|\widetilde{Y}_{\delta,N}(1)&\|_{W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}^{p}+\|\mathcal{W}(\widetilde{Y}_{\delta,N}^{2}(1))\|_{W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}^{p}\\
&+\big\|\mathcal{W}(\widetilde{Y}_{\delta,N}^{3}(1))\big\|_{W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}}^{p}\Big]\leq C_{\varepsilon,p,\delta}<\infty,\end{split}

(4.25)

uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Furthermore,
by restricting our attention to
0\leq\delta\leq 1
,
we can choose the constant
C_{\varepsilon,p,\delta}
in (
4.25
)
to be independent of
\delta
.

(ii)
Let
0\leq\delta<\infty
. For any
\theta\in\mathbb{H}_{a}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{I}_{\delta}(\theta)(1)\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}\lesssim\int_{0}^{1}\|\theta(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}dt,

where
\mathbb{H}_{a}
denotes the collection of drifts,
which are progressively measurable processes
belonging to

L^{2}([0,1];L^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{T}))
,
\mathbb{P}
-almost surely,
as in Subsection
3.4
.

The proof of Lemma
4.9

follows exactly as in the proof of Lemma
3.15
,
using the lower bounds
(
2.7
)
and (
2.8
) of
L_{\delta}(n)

(in place of (
2.2
) and (
2.3
)).
We omit details.

We conclude this section
by recalling
Proposition
4.7
implies
Proposition
4.6
,
which in turn implies Theorem
1.5

for
k=2
.

5.
Dynamical problem

In this section, we study the dynamical problem
associated with the Gibbs measures constructions in the previous sections.
In the following, we only
consider the deep-water regime
0<\delta\leq\infty
(namely, we work on the unscaled problem
(
1.1
))
and
present the proof of Theorem
1.8

since Theorem
1.10
in the shallow-water regime (
0\leq\delta<\infty
) follows
from a similar argument.
Our main strategy is to use a compactness
argument as in
[
18
,
74
,
69
]
.
In fact,
as mentioned in Section
1
,
the proof of Theorem
1.8
(i)
follows from exactly the same argument as that presented in

[
74
, Section 5]
.
As for the dynamical convergence result in Theorem
1.8
(ii),
we can repeat the same argument
but with one key additional ingredient:
the uniform (in
\delta
and
N
)
integrability of the (truncated) densities
(Proposition
3.6
).
For conciseness of the
presentation,
we restrict our attention to
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
in the following
and discuss the proof of Theorem
1.8
.
For each fixed
0<\delta<2
,
the same argument (without uniformity in
\delta
) applies to yield Theorem
1.8
(i).

In the remaining part of this section,
fix
k\in 2\mathbb{N}+1

and
s<0
.
The
k=2
case follows from exactly the same
argument by replacing
the truncated Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta,N}
in (
1.45
)
and the Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta}
in (
1.48
)
by
\rho_{\delta,N}
in (
1.49
)
and
\rho_{\delta}
in (
1.50
), respectively,
and thus we omit details.
In Subsection
5.1
,
we first study the truncated gILW equation
(
1.70
)
and
construct global-in-time invariant Gibbs dynamics
associated with the truncated Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta,N}
in (
1.45
)
for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
;
see Lemma
5.1
below.
This allows us to construct a probability measure

\nu_{\delta,N}=\rho_{\delta,N}\circ\Phi_{\delta,N}^{-1}

on space-time functions
as the pushforward of the truncated Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta,N}

under the solution map
\Phi_{\delta,N}
for the truncated gILW equation (
1.70
).
Then, by using the uniform (in
\delta
and
N
) bound
on the (truncated) densities
(Proposition
3.6
),
we prove that

\{\nu_{\delta,N}\}_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty,N\in\mathbb{N}}

is tight (Proposition
5.2
).
The main new point in this work is that we prove tightness

not only in the frequency cutoff parameter
N\in\mathbb{N}

but also in the depth parameter
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.

In Subsection
5.2
,
we then present the proof of Theorem
1.8

by constructing the limiting dynamics.
For each fixed
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
,
we can simply repeat the argument in
[
18
,
74
,
69
]
,
based on the Skorokhod representation theorem (Lemma
2.15
),
and construct the limiting invariant Gibbs dynamics (without uniqueness) as
N\to\infty
,
yielding Theorem
1.8
(̇i).
As for proving Theorem
1.8
(̇ii),
by exploiting the tightness
of
\{\nu_{\delta,N}\}_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty,N\in\mathbb{N}}
,
we use a diagonal argument
together with the triangle inequality
for
the Lévy-Prokhorov metric, characterizing weak convergence,
to show that there exists a sequence
\{\delta_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
,
tending to
\infty
,
such that
u_{\delta_{m}}
converges almost surely to some limit
u
in
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
.
Here, in order to have the claimed almost sure convergence
of
u_{\delta_{m}}
to
u
,
we apply the Skorokhod representation theorem (Lemma
2.15
).
Furthermore, in order to show that
u_{\delta_{m}}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
satisfies the renormalized gILW equation (
1.66
),
we need to apply
the Skorokhod representation theorem (Lemma
2.15
)
infinitely many times (i.e. once for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
).

5.1.
Pushforward of the truncated Gibbs measure

Given
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
consider the truncated gILW equation (
1.70
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}u_{\delta,N}-\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}u_{\delta,N}&=F_{N}(u_{\delta,N})\\
&=\partial_{x}\mathbf{P}_{N}\mathcal{W}((\mathbf{P}_{N}u_{\delta,N})^{k})\\
&=\partial_{x}\mathbf{P}_{N}H_{k}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u_{\delta,N};\sigma_{\delta,N}),\end{split}

(5.1)

where
\sigma_{\delta,N}
is as in (
1.43
)
and
F_{N}
is as in (
3.18
).
We first prove global well-posedness of (
5.1
)
and invariance of the truncated Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta,N}

defined in (
1.45
).

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and
s<0
.
Then,
the truncated gILW equation (
5.1
)
is globally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
.
Moreover, the truncated Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta,N}

is invariant under the dynamics of
(
5.1
).

Proof.

The proof of this lemma follows from that of Lemma 5.1 in
[
74
]

and thus we will be brief here.
We first decompose (
5.1
) into two parts:

\displaystyle u_{\delta,N}=u_{\delta,N}^{\textup{low}}+u_{\delta,N}^{\textup{high}}=\mathbf{P}_{N}u_{\delta,N}+\mathbf{P}_{N}^{\perp}u_{\delta,N},

(5.2)

where
\mathbf{P}_{N}^{\perp}=\textup{Id}-\mathbf{P}_{N}
.
Then,
u_{\delta,N}^{\textup{low}}
and
u_{\delta,N}^{\textup{high}}

satisfy the following equations:

(i)

nonlinear dynamics on the low-frequency part
\{0<|n|\leq N\}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u_{\delta,N}^{\textup{low}}-\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}u_{\delta,N}^{\textup{low}}=\partial_{x}\mathbf{P}_{N}H_{k}(u_{\delta,N}^{\textup{low}};\sigma_{\delta,N}).

(5.3)

(ii)

linear dynamics
on the high frequency part
\{|n|>N\}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u_{\delta,N}^{\textup{high}}-\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}u_{\delta,N}^{\textup{high}}=0.

(5.4)

We now view the equations (
5.3
) and (
5.4
)
on the Fourier side.
As a decoupled system of linear equation
(for each frequency
|n|>N
),
(
5.4
) is globally well-posed.
As for (
5.3
),
it is a system of finitely many ODEs
with a Lipschitz vector field
and thus by the Cauchy-Lipschitz theorem, it is locally well-posed.
Furthermore, a direct computation shows
that the
L^{2}
-norm of
u_{\delta,N}^{\textup{low}}

is conserved under the flow of (
5.3
),
which yields global well-posedness of (
5.3
).
Putting together, we conclude that (
5.1
) is globally well-posed.

Next, we prove invariance of the truncated Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta,N}
.
We first write

\rho_{\delta,N}
in (
1.45
) as

\displaystyle\rho_{\delta,N}=\rho_{\delta,N}^{\textup{low}}\otimes\rho^{\textup{high}}_{\delta,N},

(5.5)

where
\rho_{\delta,N}^{\textup{low}}
and
\rho^{\textup{high}}_{\delta,N}
are given as follows:

(i)

the low-frequency component

\rho_{\delta,N}^{\textup{low}}
is the finite-dimensional Gibbs measure on
\mathbf{P}_{N}H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
,
defined by

\displaystyle d\rho^{\textup{low}}_{\delta,N}(u)=Z_{\delta,N}^{-1}e^{-\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}H_{k+1}(u;\sigma_{\delta,N})dx}d\mu_{\delta,N}^{\textup{low}}(u),

where

\mu_{\delta,N}^{\textup{low}}=(\mathbf{P}_{N})_{*}\mu_{\delta}

is the pushforward image measure under
\mathbf{P}_{N}

of the
base Gaussian measure
\mu_{\delta}
in (
1.22
).
Namely,

\mu_{\delta,N}^{\textup{low}}
is the induced probability measure
under the map
\omega\in\Omega\mapsto X_{\delta,N}(\omega)=\mathbf{P}_{N}X_{\delta}(\omega)
,
where
X_{\delta}
is as in (
1.41
).

(ii)

the high-frequency component

\rho^{\textup{high}}_{\delta,N}
is nothing but the Gaussian measure

(\mathbf{P}_{N}^{\perp})_{*}\mu_{\delta}
given as the (infinite) product
of Gaussian measures at each frequency
|n|>N
:

\displaystyle(Z_{\delta,N}^{\perp})^{-1}\bigotimes_{|n|>N}e^{-\frac{1}{2\pi}K_{\delta}(n)|\widehat{u}(n)|^{2}}d\widehat{u}(n).

(5.6)

By the classical Liouville theorem
and the conservation of the (truncated) Hamiltonian
for (
5.3
),
we see that the Gibbs measure

\rho_{\delta,N}^{\textup{low}}
is invariant under the flow of (
5.3
).
On the other hand,
the linear dynamics (
5.4
) acts as a rotation
on the Fourier coefficient at each frequency
|n|>N
,
preserving the Gaussian measure
at each frequency
|n|>N
in (
5.6
).
As a result, the Gaussian measure

\rho^{\textup{high}}_{\delta,N}=(\mathbf{P}_{N}^{\perp})_{*}\mu_{\delta}

is invariant under the linear dynamics (
5.4
).
In view of (
5.2
) and (
5.5
),
we conclude invariance of the truncated Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta,N}

under the flow of the truncated gILW equation (
5.1
).
∎

As a consequence of Lemma
5.1
,
we can define the solution map
\Phi_{\delta,N}:H^{s}(\mathbb{T})\to C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))

associated to (
5.1
).
More precisely,
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
we define
\Phi_{\delta,N}(t):H^{s}(\mathbb{T})\to H^{s}(\mathbb{T})

by

\displaystyle\phi\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T})\longmapsto\Phi_{\delta,N}(t)(\phi)=u_{\delta,N}(t),

(5.7)

where
u_{\delta,N}
is the global-in-time solution to
the truncated gILW equation (
5.1
)
with initial data
u_{\delta,N}(0)=\phi
.

Next, we introduce the pushforward image measure

\nu_{\delta,N}
of the truncated Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta,N}

under the solution map
\Phi_{\delta,N}
:

\displaystyle\nu_{\delta,N}=\rho_{\delta,N}\circ\Phi_{\delta,N}^{-1}.

(5.8)

Here, we view

\nu_{\delta,N}
as a probability measure
on
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
endowed with the compact-open topology,
induced by the following metric:

\texttt{dist}(u,v)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}2^{-j}\frac{\|u-v\|_{C([-j,j];H^{s})}}{1+\|u-v\|_{C([-j,j];H^{s})}}.

Recall that, under this topology, a sequence
\{u_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))

converges
if and only if
it converges uniformly on
[-K,K]
for each finite
K>0
.
We also recall that the metric space
\big(C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T})),\texttt{dist}\big)

is complete and separable.
22
22

22

Recall that
the space of continuous functions from
a separable metric space
X
to another separable metric space
Y

with the compact-open topology is separable; see
[
60
]
.
See also the paper
[
46
, Corollary 3.3]
.

Then, it follows from the local Lipschitz continuity of
\Phi_{\delta,N}

that
\Phi_{\delta,N}
is continuous
from
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
into
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
,
which shows that
\nu_{\delta,N}

is a well-defined probability measure
on
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
endowed with the compact-open topology.
Note that we have

\displaystyle\int_{C(\mathbb{R};H^{s})}F(u)d\nu_{\delta,N}(u)=\int_{H^{s}}F(\Phi_{\delta,N}(\phi))d\rho_{\delta,N}(\phi)

(5.9)

for any bounded measurable function
F:C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))\to\mathbb{R}
.

Our main goal in this subsection is to
prove the following tightness result
on
\{\nu_{\delta,N}\}_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty,N\in\mathbb{N}}
.
We point out that tightness holds not only over
N\in\mathbb{N}

but also over
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
,
which is the key new feature of this proposition.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
s<0
.
Then,
the family
\{\nu_{\delta,N}\}_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty,N\in\mathbb{N}}

of probability measures on

C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))

is tight, and hence is relatively compact.

Before proceeding to the proof of Proposition
5.2
,
we state two auxiliary lemmas.
The first lemma
establishes
uniform (in
\delta
and
N
) space-time bounds
on the solutions to the truncated gILW equation (
5.1
).
We postpone its proof to the end of this subsection.

Given
1\leq p\leq\infty
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
we define the space
W^{1,p}_{T}H^{s}_{x}=W^{1,p}([-T,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
by the norm:

\|u\|_{W^{1,p}_{T}H^{s}_{x}}=\|u\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{s}_{x}}+\|\partial_{t}u\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{s}_{x}}.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
s<0
,
and fix finite
p\geq 1
.
Then, there exists
C_{p}>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\big\|\|u\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{s}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}(d\nu_{\delta,N})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}T^{\frac{1}{p}},

(5.10)

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\big\|\|u\|_{W^{1,p}_{T}H^{s-2}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}(d\nu_{\delta,N})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}T^{\frac{1}{p}},

(5.11)

The following interpolation lemma
allows us to control the Hölder regularity (in time)
by the two quantities controlled in Lemma
5.3
above.
For
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
, define the space

\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{T}H^{s}_{x}=\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}([-T,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
by the norm

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{T}H^{s}_{x}}=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{1},t_{2}\in[-T,T]\\
t_{1}\neq t_{2}\end{subarray}}\frac{\|u(t_{1})-u(t_{2})\|_{H^{s}}}{|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{\alpha}}+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}_{x}}.

(5.12)

Lemma 5.4

(
[
18
, Lemma 3.3]
)
.

Let
T>0
and
1\leq p\leq\infty
.
Suppose that

u\in L^{p}_{T}H^{s_{1}}_{x}
and
\partial_{t}u\in L^{p}_{T}H^{s_{2}}_{x}

for some
s_{2}\leq s_{1}
.
Then, for
\delta>p^{-1}(s_{1}-s_{2})
, we have

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s_{1}-\delta}_{x}}\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{s_{1}}}^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\|u\|_{W^{1,p}_{T}H^{s_{2}}_{x}}^{\frac{1}{p}}.

(5.13)

Moreover, there exist
\alpha>0
and
\theta\in[0,1]

such that for all
t_{1},t_{2}\in[-T,T]
, we have

\displaystyle\|u(t_{2})-u(t_{1})\|_{H^{s_{1}-2\delta}}\lesssim|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{\alpha}\|u\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{s_{1}}_{x}}^{1-\theta}\|u\|_{W^{1,p}_{T}H^{s_{2}}_{x}}^{\theta}.

(5.14)

As a consequence, we have

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{T}H^{s_{1}-2\delta}_{x}}\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{s_{1}}_{x}}+\|u\|_{W^{1,p}_{T}H^{s_{2}}_{x}}.

(5.15)

Proof.

As for (
5.13
) and (
5.14
),
see the proof of Lemma 3.3 in
[
18
]
.
The bound (
5.15
) follows from (
5.12
), (
5.13
),
and (
5.14
) with Young’s inequality.
∎

We now
present the proof of Proposition
5.2
.

Proof of Proposition
5.2
.

Let
s<s_{1}<s_{2}<0

and
\alpha\in(0,1)
.
By the Arzelà-Ascoli theorem,
the embedding

\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}([-T,T];H^{s_{1}}(\mathbb{T}))\subset C([-T,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
is compact
for each
T>0
.
From
Lemma
5.4

(with large
p\gg 1
)
and
Lemma
5.3
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\big\|\|u\|_{C^{\alpha}_{T}H^{s_{1}}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}(d\nu_{\delta,N})}\\
&\quad\lesssim\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\big\|\|u\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{s_{2}}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}(d\nu_{\delta,N})}+\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\big\|\|u\|_{W^{1,p}_{T}H^{s_{2}-2}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}(d\nu_{\delta,N})}\\
&\quad\leq C_{p}T^{\frac{1}{p}}.\end{split}

(5.16)

Given
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
define

K_{\varepsilon}
by setting

\displaystyle K_{\varepsilon}:=\big\{u\in C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T})):\,\|u\|_{C^{\alpha}_{T_{j}}H^{s_{1}}_{x}}\leq C_{0}\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{p}}T_{j}^{1+\frac{1}{p}}\hskip 5.69054pt\text{ for all }j\in\mathbb{N}\big\},

(5.17)

where
T_{j}=2^{j}
.
Then, by Chebyshev’s inequality
and (
5.16
), we have

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\nu_{\delta,N}(K_{\varepsilon}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\nu_{\delta,N}\Big(\|u\|_{C^{\alpha}_{T_{j}}H^{s_{1}}_{x}}>C_{0}\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{p}}T_{j}^{1+\frac{1}{p}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}^{-p}\varepsilon\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}T_{j}^{-p-1}\big\|\|u\|_{C^{\alpha}_{T_{j}}H^{s_{1}}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}(d\nu_{\delta,N})}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(C_{0}^{-p}C_{p}^{p}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}T_{j}^{-p}\bigg)\varepsilon<\varepsilon,

where the last step follows from choosing

C_{0}>0
sufficiently large
in the definition (
5.17
) of
K_{\varepsilon}
.

It remains to show that
K_{\varepsilon}
is compact in

\big(C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T})),\texttt{dist}\big)
,
namely, endowed with the compact-open topology.
While the proof of this fact was presented in
the proof of Proposition 5.4 in
[
74
]
,
we present the argument for readers’ convenience.
Let
\{u_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset K_{\varepsilon}
.
It follows from (
5.17
)
that

\{u_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded in

\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}([-T_{j},T_{j}];H^{s_{1}}(\mathbb{T}))
for each
j\in\mathbb{N}

and hence is compact
in
C([-T_{j},T_{j}];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then, by a diagonal argument,
we can extract a subsequence
\{u_{n_{\ell}}\}_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}

that is convergent in
C([-T_{j},T_{j}];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
.
Hence,
\{u_{n_{\ell}}\}_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}

is convergent in
\big(C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T})),\texttt{dist}\big)
.
This proves that
K_{\varepsilon}
is relatively
compact in
\big(C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T})),\texttt{dist}\big)
.
It is clear that
K_{\varepsilon}
is closed as well,
and hence we conclude the proof.
∎

We conclude this subsection by presenting the proof of Lemma
5.3
.

Proof of Lemma
5.3
.

The proof essentially follows the same lines in the proof of Lemma 5.5
in
[
74
]
.
From (
5.9
), the invariance of
\rho_{\delta,N}
under the
truncated gILW dynamics (
5.1
),
Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequality,
Proposition
3.4
(see (
3.12
) with
k=1
),
and
Proposition
3.6
(see (
3.33
)),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big\|\|u\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{s}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}(d\nu_{\delta,N})}&=\big\|\|\Phi_{\delta,N}(t)\phi\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{s}}\big\|_{L^{p}(d\rho_{\delta,N})}\\
&=\big\|\|\Phi_{\delta,N}(t)\phi\|_{L^{p}(d\rho_{\delta,N})H^{s}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}_{T}}\\
&\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{p}}\|\phi\|_{L^{p}(d\rho_{\delta,N})H^{s}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{p}}\big\|\|u\|_{H^{s}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{2p}(d\mu_{\delta,N})}Z_{\delta,N}^{-\frac{1}{p}}\big\|G_{\delta,N}(u)\big\|_{L^{2p}(d\mu_{\delta,N})}\\
&\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{p}},\end{split}

(5.18)

uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
This proves (
5.10
).

Next, we prove the second bound (
5.11
).
By writing
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}=(\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x})\partial_{x}
,
it follows from (
1.42
) and Lemma
2.1

that

\displaystyle\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}f\|_{H^{s-2}}\leq\|f\|_{H^{s}}.

(5.19)

Then,
from (
5.1
)
and (
5.19
),
we have

\displaystyle\big\|\|

\displaystyle u\|_{W^{1,p}_{T}H^{s-2}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}(d\nu_{\delta,N})}=\big\|\|\partial_{t}u\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{s-2}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}(d\nu_{\delta,N})}

\displaystyle\leq\big\|\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}u\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{s-2}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}(d\nu_{\delta,N})}+\big\|\|F_{N}(u)\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{s-2}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}(d\nu_{\delta,N})}

\displaystyle\leq\big\|\|u\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{s}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}(d\nu_{\delta,N})}+\big\|\|F_{N}(u)\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{s-1}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{p}(d\nu_{\delta,N})},

uniformly in
2\leq\delta\leq\infty

and
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
where
F_{N}(u)
is as in (
3.18
).
Then, the rest follows as in (
5.18
) from Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequality,
Proposition
3.6
,
and
Proposition
3.4
(see (
3.12
) and (
3.19
)).
∎

5.2.
Proof of Theorem
1.8

In this subsection, we present the proof of Theorem
1.8
.
We first work with fixed
2\leq\delta\leq\infty

and construct invariant Gibbs dynamics
to the renormalized gILW equation
(
1.66
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}u_{\delta}-\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}u_{\delta}&=F(u_{\delta})\\
&=\partial_{x}\mathcal{W}(u_{\delta}^{k})\end{split}

(5.20)

with the understanding that it corresponds to the renormalized
gBO equation (
1.69
) when
\delta=\infty
,
where
F(u)
is the limit of
F_{N}(u)
in (
3.18
)
constructed in
Proposition
3.4
(ii).
In view of Proposition
5.2
,
the family
\{\nu_{\delta,N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

is tight.
Hence, by the Prokhorov theorem (Lemma
2.14
),
there exists a
subsequence

\{\nu_{\delta,N_{j}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

converging
weakly to some limit,
23
23

23

The space
\mathcal{M}=C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
endowed with the compact-open
topology is complete and separable,
and thus
\mathcal{P}(M)=
the set of all the
probability measures on
\mathcal{M}
is complete;
see, for example,
[
10
, Theorem 6.8 on p. 73]
.

denoted by
\nu_{\delta}
.
Namely, we have

\displaystyle d_{\rm LP}\big(\nu_{\delta,N_{j}},\nu_{\delta}\big)\longrightarrow 0

(5.21)

as
j\to\infty
,
where
d_{\rm LP}
denotes the Lévy-Prokhorov metric
defined in (
2.42
).

By the Skorokhod representation theorem
(Lemma
2.15
),
there exist some probability space
(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\delta},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\delta},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\delta})

and
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
-valued random variables
u_{\delta,N_{j}}
and
u_{\delta}
,
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(u_{\delta,N_{j}})=\nu_{\delta,N_{j}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{L}(u_{\delta})=\nu_{\delta},

(5.22)

and
u_{\delta,N_{j}}
converges
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\delta}
-almost surely to
u_{\delta}

in
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
as
j\to\infty
.
By repeating the argument in
[
18
,
74
,
69
]

(see, in particular, Subsection 5.3 in
[
74
]
),
we obtain the following global existence result
for the gILW equation (
5.20
)
with the Gibbsian initial data
(Theorem
1.8
(i)).

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
u_{\delta,N_{j}}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
, and
u_{\delta}
be as above.
Then,

u_{\delta,N_{j}}
and
u_{\delta}

are global-in-time distributional
solutions to
the truncated gILW equation (
5.1
)
and the renormalized gILW equation (
5.20
), respectively.
Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\big(u_{\delta,N_{j}}(t)\big)=\rho_{\delta,N_{j}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{L}\big(u_{\delta}(t)\big)=\rho_{\delta}

(5.23)

for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

While the proof of Proposition
5.5
follows
exactly
the same lines in Subsection 5.3 of
[
74
]
,
we present details (with some modifications from
[
74
]
) for readers’ convenience.
We also point out that Proposition
5.5

will be applied iteratively in the proof of Theorem
1.8
(ii)
presented below.

Fix
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
Let
R_{t}:C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))\to H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
be the evaluation map defined by
R_{t}(v)=v(t)
.
Note that

R_{t}
is a continuous function.
Then, from
(
5.8
) and the invariance of the truncated Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta,N}

(Lemma
5.1
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \nu_{\delta,N}\circ R_{t}^{-1}&=\rho_{\delta,N}\circ\Phi_{\delta,N}^{-1}\circ R_{t}^{-1}=\rho_{\delta,N}\circ\big(R_{t}\circ\Phi_{\delta,N})^{-1}\\
&=\big(R_{t}\circ\Phi_{\delta,N})_{*}\,\rho_{\delta,N}=\big(\Phi_{\delta,N}(t)\big)_{*}\,\rho_{\delta,N}\\
&=\rho_{\delta,N}.\end{aligned}

(5.24)

Then,
it follows from (
5.22
) and (
5.24
) that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\big(u_{\delta,N_{j}}(t)\big)=\nu_{\delta,N_{j}}\circ R_{t}^{-1}=\rho_{\delta,N_{j}}.

(5.25)

By the construction,

u_{\delta,N_{j}}
converges to
u_{\delta}
in
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
almost surely
with respect to
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\delta}
.
Thus, we have

u_{\delta,N_{j}}(t)=R_{t}\big(u_{\delta,N_{j}}\big)\longrightarrow u_{\delta}(t)=R_{t}(u_{\delta})

almost surely as
j\to\infty
,
which in particular implies

u_{\delta,N_{j}}(t)
converges in law to

u_{\delta}(t)
as
j\to\infty
.
Namely,

\mathcal{L}\big(u_{\delta,N_{j}}(t)\big)

converges weakly to

\mathcal{L}\big(u_{\delta}(t)\big)

as
j\to\infty
.
On the other hand, recall from
Theorem
1.3
(i)
that

\rho_{\delta,N_{j}}
converges to
\rho_{\delta}

in total variation
as
j\to\infty
,
which in particular implies
that
\rho_{\delta,N_{j}}
converges weakly to
\rho_{\delta}
.
Hence, in view of (
5.25
)
and the uniqueness of the limit,
we conclude

\mathcal{L}\big(u_{\delta}(t)\big)=\rho_{\delta}
.
This proves (
5.23
).

Next, we show that the random variable
u_{\delta,N_{j}}
is indeed a
global-in-time distributional solution to (
5.1
).
Given a test function
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})=C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})
,
define
V_{\varphi,j}:C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{R}))\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle V_{\varphi,j}(u)=\big|\big\langle\varphi,\partial_{t}u-\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}u-F_{N_{j}}(u)\big\rangle\big|,

(5.26)

where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denotes the
\mathcal{D}_{t,x}
-
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{t,x}

pairing.
It is easy to see that
V_{\varphi,j}
is continuous.
In view of the separability of
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})
,
let
\{\varphi_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
be a countable dense
subset of
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})
.
Then, in view of
(
5.9
), (
5.26
), and the definition (
5.7
) of
\Phi_{\delta,N_{j}}
, we have

\displaystyle\|V_{\varphi_{m},j}\|_{L^{1}(d\nu_{\delta,N_{j}})}=\int_{H^{s}}|V_{\varphi_{m},j}(\Phi_{\delta,N_{j}}(\phi))|d\rho_{\delta,N_{j}}(\phi)=0

(5.27)

for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Namely, there exists a set
\Sigma_{m}\subset C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
such that

\nu_{\delta,N_{j}}(\Sigma_{m})=1

and
V_{\varphi_{m},j}(u)=0
for any
u\in\Sigma_{m}
.
Now, set
\Sigma=\bigcap_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\Sigma_{m}
.
Then, we have

\nu_{\delta,N_{j}}(\Sigma)=1

and, moreover,
V_{\varphi,j}(u)=0

for any
u\in\Sigma

and
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})
,
where the latter claim follows from (
5.27
) and the density of
\{\varphi_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Finally, we prove that the random variable
u_{\delta}
is a
global-in-time distributional solution to (
5.20
).
It follows from
the almost sure convergence
of
u_{\delta,N_{j}}
to
u_{\delta}
in
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))

that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u_{\delta,N_{j}}-\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}u_{\delta,N_{j}}\longrightarrow\partial_{t}u_{\delta}-\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}u_{\delta}

(5.28)

in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})
,

\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\delta}
-almost surely, as
j\to\infty
.

Next, we show almost sure convergence
of the truncated nonlinearity

F_{N_{j}}(u_{\delta,N_{j}})

to
F(u_{\delta})=\partial_{x}\mathcal{W}(u_{\delta})
.
Given
M\in\mathbb{N}
, write

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} F_{N_{j}}(u_{\delta,N_{j}})-F(u_{\delta})&=\big(F_{N_{j}}(u_{\delta,N_{j}})-F_{M}(u_{\delta,N_{j}})\big)+\big(F_{M}(u_{\delta,N_{j}})-F_{M}(u_{\delta})\big)\\
&\quad+\big(F_{M}(u_{\delta})-F(u_{\delta})\big).\end{aligned}

(5.29)

Noting that

u\in C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))\mapsto F_{M}(u)\in C(\mathbb{R};H^{s-1}(\mathbb{T}))

is continuous,
it follows from
the almost sure convergence
of
u_{\delta,N_{j}}
to
u_{\delta}
in
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))

that

F_{M}(u_{\delta,N_{j}})\longrightarrow F_{M}(u_{\delta})

in
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s-1}(\mathbb{T}))
,

\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\delta}
-almost surely, as
j\to\infty
.
As for
the first term on the right-hand side of (
5.29
),
for fixed
T>0
,
it follows from
(
5.9
),
the invariance of the truncated Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta,N}
,
and
Proposition
3.6

that

\displaystyle\big\|

\displaystyle\|F_{N_{j}}(u_{\delta,N_{j}})-F_{M}(u_{\delta,N_{j}})\|_{L^{2}_{T}H^{s-1}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{2}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\delta})}

\displaystyle=\big\|\|F_{N_{j}}(u)-F_{M}(u)\|_{L^{2}_{T}H^{s-1}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{2}(d\nu_{\delta,N_{j}})}

\displaystyle=\big\|\|F_{N_{j}}(\phi)-F_{M}(\phi)\|_{L^{2}(d\rho_{\delta,N_{j}})H^{s-1}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{2}_{T}}

\displaystyle\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{2}}Z_{\delta,N_{j}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|G_{\delta,N_{j}}\|_{L^{4}(\Omega)}\|F_{N_{j}}(\phi)-F_{M}(\phi)\|_{L^{4}(d\mu_{\delta})H^{s-1}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{2}}\|F_{N_{j}}(\phi)-F_{M}(\phi)\|_{L^{4}(d\mu_{\delta})H^{s-1}_{x}},

where the implicit constants are independent of
N_{j}
.
By applying
Proposition
3.4
(ii),
we conclude that
the first term on the right-hand side of (
5.29
) converges to
0

in
L^{2}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\delta};L^{2}([-T,T];H^{s-1}(\mathbb{T})))

as
j,M\to\infty
.
Hence, by extracting a subsequence,
the first term on the right-hand side of (
5.29
) converges to
0

in
L^{2}([-T,T];H^{s-1}(\mathbb{T}))
,

\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\delta}
-almost surely, as
j,M\to\infty
.
A similar argument shows that, by extracting a subsequence,
the third term on the right-hand side of (
5.29
) converges to
0

in
L^{2}([-T,T];H^{s-1}(\mathbb{T}))
,

\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\delta}
-almost surely, as
M\to\infty
.

Putting all together with (
5.29
),
we conclude that, up to a subsequence,

F_{N_{j}}(u_{\delta,N_{j}})

converges to
F(u_{\delta})

in
L^{2}([-T,T];H^{s-1}(\mathbb{T}))
,

\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\delta}
-almost surely, as
j\to\infty
.
Since the choice of
T>0
was arbitrary,
we can apply this argument for
T_{m}=2^{m}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Thus, with
m=1
,
there exists a subsequence

F_{N_{j_{1}}}(u_{\delta,N_{j_{1}}})

and a set
\Sigma_{1}
of full
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\delta}
-probability
such that

F_{N_{j_{1}}}(u_{\delta,N_{j_{1}}})(\omega)

converges to
F(u_{\delta})(\omega)

in
L^{2}([-T_{1},T_{1}];H^{s-1}(\mathbb{T}))

for each
\omega\in\Sigma_{1}
as
j_{1}\to\infty
.
For each
m\geq 2
,
we can extract a further subsequence

F_{N_{j_{m}}}(u_{\delta,N_{j_{m}}})

of
F_{N_{j_{m-1}}}(u_{\delta,N_{j_{m-1}}})

and a subset
\Sigma_{m}\subset\Sigma_{m-1}

of full
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\delta}
-probability
such that

F_{N_{j_{m}}}(u_{\delta,N_{j_{m}}})(\omega)

converges to
F(u_{\delta})(\omega)

in
L^{2}([-T_{m},T_{m}];H^{s-1}(\mathbb{T}))

for each
\omega\in\Sigma_{m}
as
j_{m}\to\infty
.
By a diagonal argument,
we conclude that,
passing to a subsequence, we have

F_{N_{j}}(u_{\delta,N_{j}})

converges to
F(u_{\delta})

in
L^{2}_{t,\text{loc}}H^{s-1}(\mathbb{T})
,

\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\delta}
-almost surely, which in particular implies
that this subsequence converges
to
F(u_{\delta})

in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})
,

\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\delta}
-almost surely.
Therefore,
together with (
5.28
),
we conclude that
u_{\delta}
is a global-in-time distributional solution to (
5.20
).
∎

Finally, we present the proof of Theorem
1.8
(ii).
In the discussion at the beginning of this subsection,
we used Proposition
5.2

and
the Prokhorov theorem (Lemma
2.14
)
to conclude that,
for each fixed
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
,
there exists a sequence
N_{j}\to\infty

such that
(
5.21
) holds.
In the following, we iteratively apply this argument
for integers
\delta\geq 2

and apply a diagonal argument.

(i)

Let
\delta=2
.
Then,
it follows from Proposition
5.2
that
the family
\{\nu_{2,N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

is tight.
Hence, by the Prokhorov theorem (Lemma
2.14
),
there exists
a weakly convergent subsequence

\{\nu_{2,N_{j}^{(2)}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
.
Namely,
there exists
a probability measure
\nu_{2}
on
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
such that

d_{\rm LP}(\nu_{2,N_{j}^{(2)}},\nu_{2})\to 0

as
j\to\infty
.

(ii)

For
\delta=3
,
we apply the same argument to

\{\nu_{3,N_{j}^{(2)}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

to conclude that
there exists a weakly convergent subsequence

\{\nu_{3,N_{j}^{(3)}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\{N_{j}^{(3)}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\{N_{j}^{(2)}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
.
Namely,
there exists
a probability measure
\nu_{3}
on
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
such that

d_{\rm LP}(\nu_{3,N_{j}^{(3)}},\nu_{3})\to 0

as
j\to\infty
.

(iii)

We iterate this procedure for each integer
\delta\geq 4

and construct a
weakly convergent subsequence

\{\nu_{\delta,N_{j}^{(\delta)}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\{N_{j}^{(\delta)}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\{N_{j}^{(\delta-1)}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
.
Namely,
there exists
a probability measure
\nu_{\delta}
on
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
such that

\displaystyle d_{\rm LP}(\nu_{\delta,N_{j}^{(\delta)}},\nu_{\delta})\longrightarrow 0,

(5.30)

as
j\to\infty
.

(iv)

Let
\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}=\mathbb{N}\cap[2,\infty)
.
We take a diagonal sequence
\{\nu_{\delta,N^{\delta}_{j(\delta)}}\}_{\delta\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}}
,
where
j(\delta)
is chosen such that
j(\delta)
is increasing in
\delta

and

\displaystyle d_{\rm LP}(\nu_{\delta,N^{\delta}_{j(\delta)}},\nu_{\delta})\leq\frac{1}{\delta}.

(5.31)

By Proposition
5.2
and the Prokhorov theorem (Lemma
2.14
),
the family
\big\{\nu_{\delta,N_{j(\delta)}^{(\delta)}}\big\}_{\delta\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}}

is tight and thus admits a weakly convergent subsequence

\big\{\nu_{\delta_{m},N_{j(\delta_{m})}^{(\delta_{m})}}\big\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}

to some limit, which we denote by
\nu_{\infty}
.
Namely, we have

\displaystyle d_{\rm LP}(\nu_{\delta_{m},N_{j(\delta_{m})}^{(\delta_{m})}},\nu_{\infty})\longrightarrow 0,

(5.32)

as
m\to\infty
.
By the
triangle inequality for the Lévy-Prokhorov metric
d_{\rm LP}

with (
5.31
) and (
5.32
),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}d_{\rm LP}(\nu_{\delta_{m}},\nu_{\infty})&\leq d_{\rm LP}(\nu_{\delta_{m}},\nu_{\delta_{m},N_{j(\delta_{m})}^{(\delta_{m})}})+d_{\rm LP}(\nu_{\delta_{m},N_{j(\delta_{m})}^{(\delta_{m})}},\nu_{\infty})\\
&\leq\frac{1}{\delta_{m}}+d_{\rm LP}(\nu_{\delta_{m},N_{j(\delta_{m})}^{(\delta_{m})}},\nu_{\infty})\\
&\longrightarrow 0,\end{split}

(5.33)

as
m\to\infty
(and hence
\delta_{m}\to\infty
).
Hence,

\nu_{\delta_{m}}
converges weakly
to
\nu_{\infty}
as
m\to\infty
.

By the
Skorokhod representation theorem (Lemma
2.15
),
there exist a probability space
(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}})

and
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
-valued random variables
u_{\delta_{m}}
and

u
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(u_{\delta_{m}})=\nu_{\delta_{m}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{L}(u)=\nu_{\infty}

(5.34)

and
u_{\delta_{m}}

converges
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely to

u
in
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
as
m\to\infty
.

Next, we show that

u_{\delta_{m}}
is a global-in-time distributional solution to
the renormalized gILW equation (
5.20
) (with
\delta=\delta_{m}
).
It follows from (
5.30
)
and
the
Skorokhod representation theorem (Lemma
2.15
)
that
there exist a probability space
(\widetilde{\Omega}_{m},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{m},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{m})

and
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
-valued random variables
\widetilde{u}_{\delta_{m},N_{j}^{(\delta_{m})}}
and

\widetilde{u}_{\delta_{m}}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{u}_{\delta_{m},N_{j}^{(\delta_{m})}})=\nu_{\delta_{m},N_{j}^{(\delta_{m})}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{u}_{\delta_{m}})=\nu_{\delta_{m}}

(5.35)

and
\widetilde{u}_{\delta_{m},N_{j}^{(\delta_{m})}}

converges
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{m}
-almost surely to

\widetilde{u}_{\delta_{m}}
in
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
as
j\to\infty
.
Arguing as in the proof of Proposition
5.5
,
we see that

\widetilde{u}_{\delta_{m}}

is a global-in-time distributional
solution to
the renormalized gILW equation (
5.20
).
Hence, from (
5.34
)
and (
5.35
),
we conclude that

u_{\delta_{m}}

is a global-in-time distributional
solution to
the renormalized gILW equation (
5.20
).

It remains to show that
u
satisfies
the renormalized gBO equation (
1.69
) in the
distributional sense.
The almost sure convergence of
u_{\delta_{m}}

to
u
implies that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}{u_{\delta_{m}}}-\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}{u_{\delta_{m}}}\longrightarrow\partial_{t}u-\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}^{2}u

(5.36)

in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})
as
m\to\infty
.
Next, we discuss convergence of the nonlinearity.
Let
F(u_{\delta})=\partial_{x}\mathcal{W}(u_{\delta}^{k})

be as in Proposition
3.4
(ii).
Given
M\in\mathbb{N}
, write

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} F(u_{\delta_{m}})-F(u)&=\big(F(u_{\delta_{m}})-F_{M}(u_{\delta_{m}})\big)+\big(F_{M}(u_{\delta_{m}})-F_{M}(u)\big)\\
&\quad+\big(F_{M}(u)-F(u)\big),\end{aligned}

(5.37)

From
the continuity of
F_{M}

and
the almost sure convergence of
u_{\delta_{m}}

to
u
,
we see that the second term on the right-hand side of (
5.37
)
tends to
0

in
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s-1}(\mathbb{T}))
,

\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely,
as
m\to\infty
.
As for the first and third terms on the right-hand side of (
5.29
),
we need to exploit the uniform (in
\delta
and
N
) bounds,
which is the main difference from the proof of Proposition
5.5

presented above.
Let
T>0
.
Then, from
(
5.9
)
and the invariance of the truncated Gibbs measure
\rho_{\delta,N}
,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \big\|&\|F(u_{\delta_{m}})-F_{M}(u_{\delta_{m}})\|_{L^{2}_{T}H^{s-1}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{2}(\widetilde{\Omega})}\\
&=\big\|\|F(u)-F_{M}(u)\|_{L^{2}_{T}H^{s-1}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{2}(d\nu_{\delta_{m}})}\\
&=\big\|\|F(\phi)-F_{M}(\phi)\|_{L^{2}(d\rho_{\delta_{m}})H^{s-1}_{x}}\big\|_{L^{2}_{T}}\\
&\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{2}}Z_{\delta_{m}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|G_{\delta_{m}}\|_{L^{4}(\Omega)}\|F(\phi)-F_{M}(\phi)\|_{L^{4}(d\mu_{\delta_{m}})H^{s-1}_{x}}\end{aligned}

(5.38)

with the understanding that

u_{\delta_{\infty}}=u

when
m=\infty
.
From Proposition
3.6
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sup_{m\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}}Z_{\delta_{m}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}+\sup_{m\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}}\|G_{\delta_{m}}\|_{L^{4}(\Omega)}&\lesssim 1.\end{split}

(5.39)

Then, from (
5.38
), (
5.39
), and Proposition
3.4
(ii)
(see (
3.20
) with
(M,N)=(\infty,N)
),
we conclude that
the first and third terms on the right-hand side of (
5.37
) converge to
0

in
L^{2}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\delta};L^{2}([-T,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{T})))

as
M\to\infty
.
Then, by first taking
m\to\infty
and then
M\to\infty
in (
5.37
),
we conclude that, by extracting a subsequence,

F(u_{\delta_{m}})
converges to
F(u)

in
L^{2}([-T,T];H^{s-1}(\mathbb{T}))
,
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely,
as
m\to\infty
.
By repeating the argument at the end of the proof of Proposition

5.5
,
we see that
up to a further subsequence,

F(u_{\delta_{m}})
converges
to
F(u)

in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})
,

\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely, as
m\to\infty
.
Therefore,
together with (
5.36
),
we conclude that
u
is a global-in-time distributional solution to
the renormalized gBO equation (
5.20
).
This concludes the proof of Theorem
1.8
(ii).

Remark 5.6
.

In this paper,
we considered probability measures on
H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})
for fixed small
\varepsilon>0
.
In the following, we briefly explain how to remove the dependence on
\varepsilon
.
First, we set

H^{0-}(\mathbb{T}):=\bigcap_{s>0}H^{-s}(\mathbb{T})=\bigcap_{j\in\mathbb{N}}H^{-s_{j}}(\mathbb{T}),

with
s_{j}=\frac{1}{j}
.
Then, we equip
H^{0-}(\mathbb{T})
with the following distance:

\textbf{d}(f,g)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}2^{-j}\frac{\|f-g\|_{H^{-s_{j}}}}{1+\|f-g\|_{H^{-s_{j}}}}.

By definition, we have
\textbf{d}(f_{n},f)\to 0
if and only if

f_{n}
converges to
f
in
H^{-s_{j}}(\mathbb{T})
for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
\mathbf{D}
be the set of smooth functions
Q\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
of the form

Q(x)=\sum_{|n|\leq N}q_{n}e_{n}(x),

with
q_{n}\in\mathbb{Q}
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then,
\mathbf{D}
is a countable
dense subset of
H^{-s_{j}}(\mathbb{T})
for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
f\in H^{0-}(\mathbb{T})
.
Then, for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
there exists
Q_{j,N}\in\mathbf{D}

such that

\|Q_{j,N}-f\|_{H^{-s_{j}}}\leq 2^{-N}.

Now, set

Q_{N}=Q_{N,N}\in\mathbf{D}
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then, given
\varepsilon>0
, by choosing
N\geq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
,
we have

\|Q_{N}-f\|_{H^{-\varepsilon}}\leq\|Q_{N}-f\|_{H^{-\frac{1}{N}}}=\|Q_{N,N}-f\|_{H^{-\frac{1}{N}}}\leq 2^{-N}.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\textbf{d}(Q_{N},f)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{N}2^{-j}\|Q_{N}-f\|_{H^{-\frac{1}{j}}}+\sum_{j=N+1}^{\infty}2^{-j}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-N}+2^{-N}\longrightarrow 0,

as
N\to\infty
.
In other words, we just proved that

\mathbf{D}
is also a countable dense subset of
H^{0-}(\mathbb{T})
with respect to the
metric
d
.
Hence, from
[
46
]
, we see that
C(\mathbb{R};H^{0-}(\mathbb{T}))
is separable.
24
24

24

Note that we have

C(\mathbb{R};H^{0-}(\mathbb{T}))=\bigcap_{j=1}^{\infty}C\big(\mathbb{R};H^{-\frac{1}{j}}(\mathbb{T})\big).

This
allows us to repeat the entire paper by replacing

C(\mathbb{R};H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}))
with

C(\mathbb{R};H^{0-}(\mathbb{T}))
.
